A pasting theorem for iterated Segal spaces
Jaco Ruit

Abstract
. We introduce a novel notion of pasting shapes for iterated Segal spaces which classify particular arrangements of composing cells in d d -uple Segal spaces. Using this formalism, we then continue to prove a pasting theorem for these iterated Segal spaces.



1.
Introduction

A
d
-uple category is a generalization of a
d
-category, introduced by Ehresmann
[
Ehr63
]
, which has
k
-cells in
d\choose k
different directions for
0\leq k\leq d
.
In such a structure, the compatible
k
-cells that point in the same direction
may be composed, while cells with different directions may be related by
(k+1)
-cells. For
d=2
and
d=3
, these are also known as
double categories

[
Ehr63
, Definition 10]
and
intercategories

[
GP15
, Section 1]
, respectively.
These
d
-uple categories are the natural place to study mathematical structures that allow for multiple sorts of morphisms (1-cells) between them.

For instance, in the realm of algebra, one can consider not only the usual maps between rings but also bimodules between rings. These are the two
directions of 1-cells in the

Morita double category
(
[
Shu11
, Example 2.3]
). Since its introduction, the theory of double categories has found a wide range of applications throughout category theory.
For instance, the theory of 2-categorical limits admits a neat description using double categories
[
GP99
]
.
Pseudo 2-functors that form a
proarrow equipment
are better understood as being double categories with additional properties
[
Ver92
]
,
[
Shu08
]
.
These proarrow equipments give rise to formal category theories.
In
[
Shu11
]
, we see an application of the theory of double categories to homotopy theory.

Whereas in a usual category,
one may consider strings of compatible morphisms and take their composites, there are now many configurations of compatible cells in a
d
-uple category.
For instance, one may start with compatible 2-cells

v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4},v_{5},v_{6}
in a double category
D
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{10}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{20}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{40}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{11}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{21}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{31}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{41}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{32}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{42}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{03}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{23}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{33}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{43},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{5}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{6}}

and wonder: does there exist a unique composite 2-cell
v
in
D
? It has been shown by Dawson and Paré
[
DP93
]
that (in particular) this arrangement of 2-cells admits
such a composite
v
. However, not every compatible arrangement
of 2-cells in a general double category admits a composite. The authors of
[
DP93
]
established that there is an arrangement that does not have a composite in a general double category, which is called the
pinwheel
(we will see this arrangement
again in
3.2
), and which is in a particular sense, the canonical example of such an inadmissible arrangement (see
[
Daw95
]
).
The procedure of obtaining new cells by composing compatible arrangements is also called
pasting
, which was first introduced by Bénabou
[
Bén67
]

in the context of 2-categories.

The practice of pasting cells occurs in the context of many different categorical structures. Hence, one would like to have access to a
pasting theorem

that asserts the existence and uniqueness of composites for certain configurations of cells in the categorical structures one considers.
A famous pasting theorem for 2-categories was formulated and proven by Power
[
Pow91
]
. Nowadays, a wide range of pasting theorems for (strict)
\omega
-categories are available. Forest
[
For22
]
has recently
unified the main pasting theorems in this context to a more general pasting theorem for
\omega
-categories. There
is a pasting theorem for double categories due to Dawson and Paré
[
DP93
]
.

The emergence of (weak)
\infty
-category theory has created a
need for variants of pasting theorems in the weaker setting. In this context, it is no longer natural to ask for unique composites,
but instead require that the space
1
1

1

By space, we will always mean an
\infty
-groupoid in this paper.
of composites is contractible. Lately, Hackney, Ozornova, Riehl and Rovelli
[
HORR21
]
have proven such a pasting
theorem for
(\infty,2)
-categories that generalizes Power’s pasting theorem for 2-categories.
There is an
\infty
-analog for double categories as well, so called
double
\infty
-categories
, introduced by Haugseng
[
Hau13
]
and further
studied by Moser
[
Mos20
]
.
Thus, one may now ask: does there exist a pasting theorem for these double
\infty
-categories?

The goal of this paper is to answer this question affirmatively. We will treat a more general pasting problem for
d
-uple categories in the weak
\infty
-categorical setting.
Note that this would already be interesting from the strict perspective:
the pasting theorem particularly yields a novel pasting theorem for
d
-uple categories. The author does not know of an existing similar result for
d>2
.

We will consider an
\infty
-categorical variant of
d
-uple categories that are called
d
-uple Segal spaces

[
Hau17
]
.
Ordinary (1-uple) Segal spaces were first introduced by Rezk
[
Rez01
]
as a model for
\infty
-categories.
The
d
-uple Segal spaces are iterated variants of these that have additional directions for morphisms. For instance, a
2
-uple Segal space
X
contains a space of objects, and between any two objects,
a space of
vertical
and
horizontal
arrows. Thus a 2-uple Segal space has two categorical directions instead of merely one as is the case for an ordinary
Segal space. Compatible arrows of
X
that have the same direction, can be composed in a coherently associative fashion. Moreover,
X
contains a space of 2-cells. A 2-cell may be pictured as a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

in
X
. Here the arrows that point horizontally are horizontal arrows of
X
and similarly for the vertical ones. That is, 2-cells have a source and target vertical arrow and a source and target horizontal arrow.
Again,
X
has a coherently associative composition for these 2-cells, which is compatible with the composition of 1-cells.
In general,
d
-uple Segal spaces contain
d
categorical directions, which may interact coherently using higher cells.

These
d
-uple Segal spaces are rich structures that
play a useful role in
\infty
-category theory. For instance, by ‘truncating’ all but one of the categorical directions, they can be used to model
(\infty,d)
-categories. This
is the model for
(\infty,d)
-categories due to Barwick
[
Bar05
]
, which we will briefly discuss in
5
. It has been (directly) compared to other
models for
(\infty,d)
-categories by Bergner and Rezk in
[
BR13
]
and
[
BR20
]
, and Loubaton, Ozornova and Rovelli in
[
Lou22
]
and
[
OR22
]
.
Consequently,
d
-uple Segal spaces may act as a useful intermediate step towards constructing
(\infty,d)
-categories. For instance, in order to construct
(\infty,d)
-categories of iterated
spans
or to construct the
Morita
(\infty,d)
-categories
it is more convenient to define their encompassing
d
-uple Segal spaces first (see
[
Hau18
]
and
[
Hau17
]
).
We also believe that
d
-uple Segal spaces can be used to study phenomena of
(\infty,d)
-categories. For instance, the universal property of the
(\infty,2)
-category
of spans
[
EH23
]
should be the shadow of a universal property of
2
-uple Segal space of spans that is
analogous to one in the strict case
[
DPP10
]
. We hope to study this in future work.

This work grew out of the author’s study of double
\infty
-categories, which can be viewed
as 2-uple Segal spaces that satisfy a
completeness
assumption (see
2.2
). More specifically, out
of the interest in a particular class of double
\infty
-categories: so-called
\infty
-equipments
, which are generalizations of proarrow equipments to the
\infty
-categorical context.
These
\infty
-equipments offer a context for
synthetic
or
formal category theory
.
There exist suitable
\infty
-equipments for equivariant, indexed, internal, fibered, enriched and ordinary
\infty
-category theory. We commenced the study of these
\infty
-equipments in
[
Rui23
]
.

Content of the paper

We will commence the paper by setting up the necessary preliminaries, including revisiting the definition of iterated Segal spaces, in
2
.

Subsequently, in
3
, we introduce the novel notion of
d
-dimensional pasting shapes
whose
nerves
classify arrangements of cells in a
d
-uple Segal space.
For
d=3
, these include arrangements of rectangular cuboids, whose faces may be subdivided into smaller rectangles.
The faces of these rectangles may in turn be subdivided into smaller edges. We will also discuss
some fundamental properties that pasting shapes may have. In particular, we single out an important class
of pasting shapes: the so-called
(locally) composable
ones. The composable
pasting shapes are certain well-behaved pasting shapes. For

d=2
, the aforementioned pinwheel of Dawson and Paré is an example of a pasting shape that is not composable.

After setting up the theory of pasting shapes, we can formulate the main result of this paper, the
pasting theorem
for
d
-uple Segal spaces:

Theorem A

(The pasting theorem)
.

Suppose that
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}
is a covering of a
d
-dimensional locally composable pasting shape
I
. Then
I
can be written as a union

I=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}I_{i},

and this colimit description is preserved when passing to the
\infty
-category of
d
-uple Segal spaces via the nerve functor for pasting shapes.

The precise statement appears as
3.49
in the paper.
This theorem can be used to show
3.3
, which states that the
spine inclusion
associated with a composable pasting shape is an equivalence.
The composable pasting shapes thus classify arrangements of cells that admit a composite that is unique up to contractible choice (see
3.3
).

The technical heart of the paper is in
4
. Here,
we proceed by induction on the dimension of pasting shapes,
to give a proof of
A
.
After this demonstration, we conclude the paper in
5
by giving an idea of how our pasting theorem for
d
-uple Segal spaces may yield
a pasting theorem for
(\infty,d)
-categories.

Conventions

We will use the language of
\infty
-categories throughout this article.
For definiteness, we work with the model of quasi-categories for
\infty
-categories as developed by Joyal and Lurie
[
Lur09a
]
. The readers that prefer to use the language of model categories, may interpret the main results of this paper within the appropriate model categories associated with the
\infty
-categories in question. We will use
the following customary notation:

•

The (large)
\infty
-categories of spaces
[
Lur09a
, Definition 1.2.16.1]
and
\infty
-categories
[
Lur09a
, Definition 3.0.0.1]
are denoted by
\mathscr{S}
and
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
, respectively.

•

If
\mathscr{C}
is an
\infty
-category, we write

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,-):\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

for its associated mapping space functor
[
Lur09a
, Subsection 5.1.3]
.

•

Every
1
-category
\mathscr{C}
will be viewed as an
\infty
-category, suppressing the notation of the nerve
[
Lur09a
, Subsection 1.1.2]
.

2.
Preliminaries

A clear definition of the (strict)
d
-uple categories introduced by Ehresmann
[
Ehr63
]
can be given in terms of
Grothendieck’s notion of
categorical objects

[
Gro95
]
.
Following
[
Hau17
]
and
[
Hau18
]
, we will introduce an
\infty
-categorical variant on
d
-uple categories via the same principle.

2.1.
Categorical objects

To this end, we first need the notion of categorical objects in an
\infty
-category.
Throughout this section, we will fix an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
and assume that it has all pullbacks.
The following definition is due to
[
Lur09b
]
:

Definition 2.1
.

A
categorical object
X
in
\mathscr{C}
is a simplicial object
X:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}

such that the so-called
Segal map

X([n])\rightarrow X(\{0\leq 1\})\times_{X(\{1\})}\dotsb\times_{X(\{n-1\})}X(\{n-1\leq n\})

is an equivalence for all
n
. The full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})
spanned by the categorical objects in
\mathscr{C}
is denoted by
\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{C})
.

Example 2.2
.

We have the following examples:

•

A categorical object in the
(2,1)
-category of categories is a pseudo double category. These were defined by Grandis and Paré
[
GP99
]
.

•

If
\mathscr{C}
is given by the
\infty
-category of spaces
\mathscr{S}
, then
\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{S})
is the
\infty
-category that underlies

the model category of Segal spaces
, constructed by Rezk in
[
Rez01
, Theorem 7.1]
. Henceforth, we will refer to categorical
objects in
\mathscr{S}
as
Segal spaces
.

•

A categorical object in
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
is called a
double
\infty
-category
. These were first studied by Haugseng in
[
Hau13
]
.

Note that the subcategory

\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})
is closed under limits, so that limits of categorical objects may be computed pointwise.
In particular, we deduce that
\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{C})
again admits all pullbacks. Thus, we may iterate
2.1
:

Definition 2.3
.

We define the
\infty
-category of
d
-uple categorical objects in
\mathscr{C}
by

\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{C}):=\textup{Cat}(\dotsb\textup{Cat}(\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{C}))\dotsb).

By adjunction, the
\infty
-category of
d
-uple categorical objects in
\mathscr{C}
can be described
as a full subcategory of the
\infty
-category

\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{C})

of
d
-uple simplicial objects in
\mathscr{C}
. In the case that
\mathscr{C}
is presentable, then

\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{C})
is a (left) reflective subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{C})
. I.e. the
inclusion admits a left adjoint (see also
[
Lur09a
, Remark 5.2.7.9]
) in this case. We will demonstrate how this can be established.

Construction 2.4
.

Let us write

\Delta[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]:\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d}\rightarrow\textup{Set}\subset\mathscr{S}

for the discrete presheaf represented by
([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])\in\Delta^{\times d}
.
We consider the category
\mathbb{G}^{\times d}/([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])
defined by the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}^{\times d}/([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\times d}_{\mathrm{in}}/([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}^{\times d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{in}}^{\times d}.}

Here
\Delta_{\mathrm{in}}
is the subcategory of
\Delta
spanned by the
inert
maps: i.e. maps that are injective and have convex image.
The category
\mathbb{G}
is in turn the full
subcategory of
\Delta_{\mathrm{in}}
spanned by
[0]
and
[1]
, the free 0- and 1-cells.
The
spine inclusion
for
([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])
is now defined to be the map of presheaves

i_{n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}}:\colim_{([k_{1}],\dotsc,[k_{d}])\in\mathbb{G}^{\times d}/([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])}\Delta[k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d}]\rightarrow\Delta[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}].

We will see a more geometrically flavored definition of the spine inclusions in
3
.

Proposition 2.5
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is presentable, then there exists a left adjoint

L:\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{C}),

to the inclusion

\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{C})
.

Proof.

Let
G
be a (small) set of compact generators for
\mathscr{C}
, so that any object of
\mathscr{C}
can be written as the colimit of a small diagram of objects in
G
(see characterization (6) of
[
Lur09a
, Theorem 5.5.1.1]
). Then we obtain a set
S
of maps of
d
-uple simplicial
\mathscr{C}
-objects whose
elements are given by

i_{n_{1},\dotsc,n_{m}}\otimes x:\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d}\xrightarrow{(i_{n_{1},\dotsc,n_{m}},x)}\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{-\otimes-}\mathscr{C},\quad n_{1},\dotsc,n_{m}\in\mathbb{N},x\in G.

Here
-\otimes-
denotes the canonical tensor product
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
that is determined
by fitting in adjunctions

-\otimes x:\mathscr{S}\rightleftarrows\mathscr{C}:\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(x,-),

for
x\in\mathscr{C}
. This tensor product is discussed in
[
Lur09a
, Subsection 4.4.4]
. By construction,
the
S
-local objects (see
[
Lur09a
, Definition 5.5.4.1]
) are precisely the
d
-uple categorical objects in
\mathscr{C}
. Hence, the result follows from
[
Lur09a
, Proposition 5.5.4.15]
.
∎

It directly follows from the definition that
d
-uple categorical objects are functorial in the following way:

Proposition 2.6
.

Suppose that
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a finite limit preserving functor between
\infty
-categories that admit all finite limits.
Then the induced functor
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{D})
restricts
to a functor
\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{D})
.

Moreover, there is functoriality in the dimension
d
. Fix
0\leq k\leq d
and a choice of
a
k
-tuple of indices
1\leq h_{1}<\dotsc<h_{k}\leq d
. The category
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
has an initial object
given by
[0]
. Hence, the projection functor

p_{h}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times k}:([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])\mapsto([n_{h_{1}}],\dotsc,[n_{h_{k}}])

admits a (fully faithful) left adjoint
j_{h}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times k}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d}

given on objects by

j_{h}([m_{1}],\dotsc,[m_{k}])_{a}=\begin{cases}[m_{b}]&\text{if $a=h_{b}$},\\
[0]&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We now obtain an induced adjunction

p_{h}^{*}:\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times k},\mathscr{C})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{C}):j_{h}^{*}

on functor
\infty
-categories.
The following is readily verified:

Proposition 2.7
.

The adjunction
(p_{h}^{*},j_{h}^{*})
restricts to an adjunction

p_{h}^{*}:\textup{Cat}^{k}(\mathscr{C})\rightleftarrows\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{C}):j_{h}^{*}.

2.2.
Iterated Segal spaces

We now specialize the notions of iterated categorical objects to obtain a variant on
d
-uple categories (cf.
[
Hau17
, Section 3]
):

Definition 2.8
.

The
d
-uple categorical objects in the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{S}
of spaces and in the
category
Set
of sets are called
d
-uple Segal spaces
and
d
-uple Segal sets
, respectively.

The (limit-preserving) inclusion
\textup{Set}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
gives rise to an inclusion

\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\textup{Set})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S})

that further restricts to a fully faithful functor

\textup{Cat}^{d}(\textup{Set})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})

on account of
2.1
. We leave these inclusions implicit and view
d
-uple Segal sets
and
d
-uple simplicial sets as (discrete)
d
-uple Segal spaces and
d
-uple simplicial spaces, respectively.

Remark 2.9
.

Following the definition of ordinary multifold categories, a
d
-uple
\infty
-category
should be a
(d-1)
-uple categorical object in
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
.
Joyal and Tierney
[
JT07
]
have shown that
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
is a reflective subcategory of
\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{S})
,
identifying
\infty
-categories with the Segal spaces that are
complete
(see
5
for a definition and a precise statement of this fact).
This implies that

\textup{Cat}^{d-1}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty})
can be viewed as a reflective subcategory of
\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
spanned by
those
d
-uple Segal spaces that satisfy a certain additional completeness condition. Throughout this article, we prefer to work with the latter, more general, notion.

Definition 2.10
.

We say that a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
between
d
-uple simplicial spaces is a
Segal equivalence
if it is carried to an equivalence by the localization functor
L:\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
, or, equivalently if the induced map

f^{*}:\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S})}(Y,Z)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S})}(X,Z)

of spaces is an equivalence for every
d
-uple Segal space
Z
.

Remark 2.11
.

On account of
[
Lur09a
, Proposition 5.4.15]
, the class of Segal equivalences is the
strongly generated class of morphisms
(see
[
Lur09a
, Definition 5.5.4.5]
) generated
by all spine inclusions
i_{n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}}
,
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}\geq 0
.

We end this subsection with two important propositions that will be used repeatedly throughout this article to identify Segal equivalences between
d
-uple simplicial sets.

Proposition 2.12
.

Suppose that we have a pushout diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

in
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\textup{Set})
so that the map
A\rightarrow B
is injective and a Segal equivalence. Then the map
X\rightarrow Y
is injective and a Segal equivalence as well.

Proof.

Since the localization functor
L
is a left adjoint, we deduce that Segal equivalences are closed under pushouts in
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S})
.
Hence, it suffices to show that the pushout square
is preserved under the functor

\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\textup{Set})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S}).

This can be checked level-wise. The inclusion
\textup{Set}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
factors as

\textup{Set}\rightarrow\textup{sSet}\rightarrow\mathscr{S},

where the functor
\textup{sSet}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

witnesses the
\infty
-category of spaces
\mathscr{S}
as the localization of the category of simplicial sets
sSet
at the weak homotopy equivalences, i.e. it witnesses
\mathscr{S}
as the underlying
\infty
-category
of
sSet
equipped with the Kan-Quillen model structure. This localization functor carries homotopy colimits to colimits (see
[
Lur09a
, Theorem 4.2.4.1]
).
Hence, we have to show that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

is level-wise a homotopy pushout square in the Kan-Quillen model structure on
sSet
. But this follows
from the fact that
A\rightarrow B
is a level-wise cofibration, and all objects in
sSet
are cofibrant (see
[
Lur09a
, Proposition A.2.4.4]
for instance).
∎

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uple simplicial set. Suppose that
S
is
a collection
of pairs
(A,B)
where
A\subset B\subset X
are
d
-uple simplicial subsets, which
satisfies the following two conditions:

(1)

for every
(A,B)
,
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in S
, we have
A\cap B^{\prime}\subset A^{\prime}
and
(A\cap B^{\prime},B\cap B^{\prime})\in S
,

(2)

for every
(A,B)\in S
, the inclusion
A\rightarrow B
is a Segal equivalence.

Then if
(A_{1},B_{1}),\dotsc,(A_{n},B_{n})
are pairs in
S
, the inclusion

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}

is a Segal equivalence as well.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the length of the sequence. If
n=0
, then there is nothing to show. Suppose that the statement
holds for sequences of length
n-1
. Suppose that
(A_{1},B_{1}),\dotsc,(A_{n},B_{n})
are pairs in
S
.
Then we can consider the factorization

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\rightarrow B_{1}\cup\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{n}A_{i}\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}.

Note that the right map fits in a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{n}A_{i}\cap B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\cup\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{n}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{n}B_{i}\cap B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}.}

In light of the property (1) of
S
and the induction hypothesis, the left vertical map is a Segal equivalence.
Hence, the map on the right is a Segal equivalence as well on account of
2.2
.
It thus suffices to show that the map

\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\rightarrow B_{1}\cup\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{n}A_{i}

is a Segal equivalence. This fits in a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\cap B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\cup\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{n}A_{i}.}

On account of property (1), the left map is the inclusion
A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1}
, which is a Segal equivalence, so that another application of
2.2
yields
the desired result.
∎

3.
Pasting shapes and their nerves

We commence this section by introducing
pasting shapes
and their
nerves
. The remaining of this section will be devoted to studying basic properties
of these pasting shapes and introducing the pasting theorem.

Definition 3.1
.

A
(d,k)
-box
(resp.
non-degenerate
(d,k)
-box
) is a pair

(x,y)\in\mathbb{N}^{\times d}\times\mathbb{N}^{\times d}

such that
x_{a}=y_{a}
for
d-k
indices
a
, and
x_{a}\leq y_{a}
(resp.
x_{a}<y_{a}
) for the remaining
k
indices
a
. We denote the set of
(d,k)
-boxes by
B^{d,k}
.

A
(d,k-1)
-box
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is called a
face
of a
(d,k)
-box
(x,y)
if
x^{\prime}_{a},y^{\prime}_{a}\in\{x_{a},y_{a}\}
for all indices
a
.

We say that two (non-degenerate)
(d,k)
-boxes
(x,y)
,
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
are
adjacent

if
x_{a}=y_{a}=x^{\prime}_{a}=y^{\prime}_{a}
for
d-k
indices
a
, and out of the remaining
k
indices
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{k}
, there is at most one index
i
such that
x_{a_{i}}<y_{a_{i}}=x^{\prime}_{a_{i}}<y^{\prime}_{a_{i}}
and for the other indices
j
, we have
x_{a_{j}}=x^{\prime}_{a_{j}}<y^{\prime}_{a_{j}}=y_{a_{j}}
.
In this case
(x,y^{\prime})
is a non-degenerate
(d,k)
-box, which we will call the
join
of
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I
is a subset

B^{d}(I)\subset B^{d,d}

of
(d,d)
-boxes
giving rise to a chain

B^{0}(I)\subset B^{1}(I)\subset\dotsb\subset B^{d}(I),

where
B^{k}(I):=B^{d,k}\cap B^{d}(I)
are called the
k
-boxes in
I
,
satisfying the following two properties:

(1)

closure under faces:
for every box
(x,y)
in
I
, the boxes
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
such that
x^{\prime}_{a},y^{\prime}_{a}\in\{x_{a},y_{a}\}
for each index
a
, are in
I
,

(2)

closure under joins:
if
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
are adjacent
k
-boxes in
I
, then its join
(x,y^{\prime})
must be contained in
I
.

Note that
B^{0}(I)
is the diagonal of a subset
V(I)\subset\mathbb{N}^{\times d}
, which we will call the
vertices
of
I
. Any box
(x,y)
in
I
has
x,y\in V(I)
.

A
map of pasting shapes

f:I\rightarrow J
between
d
-dimensional pasting shapes
I
and
J
is a map
f:V(I)\rightarrow V(J)
between their underlying sets of vertices,
such that for any box
(x,y)
in
I
,
(f(x),f(y))
is a box in
J
with the property that

f(x)_{a}=f(y)_{a}
whenever
x_{a}=y_{a}
. With these maps, the
d
-dimensional pasting shapes form a category which we will denote by

\textup{Shape}^{d}
.

Example 3.3
.

The
1
-dimensional pasting shapes may be identified with subposets of
\mathbb{N}
.

Example 3.4
.

We may view
d
-dimensional pasting shapes as being particular graphs equipped with extra markings. For instance, for
d=2
, the following graph

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,3)}

depicts the smallest
2
-dimensional pasting shape
I^{\prime}
whose vertices are given by the vertices in the graph, and contains every 1-box
(x,y)
for which
there exists a (directed) path of edges between
x
and
y
. Note that the resulting pasting shape has no non-degenerate 2-boxes.
To remedy this, our picture needs to reflect which 2-boxes need to be included in the associated pasting shape.

In this article, we will use the convention to color the backgrounds of the 2-boxes we would like to include. Thus the following picture

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,3)}

depicts the
2
-dimensional pasting shape
I
which contains
I^{\prime}
and all the non-degenerate 2-boxes whose faces are in
I
and enclose a colored region in the picture. In this case,
these are precisely all non-degenerate 2-boxes whose faces are in
I^{\prime}
. In
4.1
, we see an example
of a colored graph where this is not the case.

For
d=3
, we can draw similar pictures. Accompanied with the data of
2
-boxes and
3
-boxes, the following graph

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,2,2)}

would define a pasting shape
J
. The graph above determines
B^{1}(J)
, but it now becomes visually intractable to color 2- and 3-boxes in the graph. Instead, we will describe the sets of 2- and 3-boxes in this case.
For instance, we may take

\displaystyle B^{3}(J)\setminus B^{2}(J)=N^{3}:=\{

\displaystyle((0,0,0),(1,2,2)),((1,0,0),(2,1,1)),((1,0,1),(2,1,2)),

\displaystyle((1,0,0),(2,1,2)),((1,1,0),(2,2,2)),((1,0,0),(2,2,2)),

\displaystyle((0,0,0),(2,2,2))\},

for the non-degenerate 3-boxes of
J
. For the set

B^{2}(J)\setminus B^{1}(J)

of non-degenerate 2-boxes of
J
, we can pick all those non-degenerate 2-boxes
(x,y)

with the property that the faces of
(x,y)
are in the graph above, and there exists a face
(\alpha,\omega)\in B^{3,2}
of a box in
N^{3}
such that
\alpha_{a}\leq x_{a},y_{a}\leq\omega_{a}
for all
a
.

Note that pasting shapes have a notion of
subshapes
,
unions
and
intersections
:

Definition 3.5
.

Let
I
be a
d
-dimensional pasting shape. A subshape
I^{\prime}\subset I
of
I
is a
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I^{\prime}
such that
B^{d}(I^{\prime})\subset B^{d}(I)
.

A subshape
E\subset I
is called a
k
-entire subshape of
I
if there there exists a non-degenerate
k
-box

(\alpha,\omega)
whose
(k-1)
-faces are in
I
, with the property that any box
(x,y)
of
I
is contained in
E
if and only if
\alpha_{a}\leq x_{a},y_{a}\leq\omega_{a}
for all
a
.
In this case, the pair
(\alpha,\omega)
is called the pair of
bounding box of
E
. We say that
E
is
closed

if
(\alpha,\omega)\in I
, in which case
(\alpha,\omega)
is also contained in
E
. If
E
is
k
-entire and every
k
-box of
E
is degenerate, then
E
is called
open
.

A
k
-entire subshape
V\subset I
is called a
k
-
vertebra
of
I
if for any
k
-entire subshape
E\subset V
, we have
E=V
.

If
k=d
, we will drop
d
from the notation, and consequently call
d
-entire subshapes and
d
-vertebrae of
I
, respectively, entire subshapes and vertebrae.

Remark 3.6
.

For a
d
-dimensional pasting shape that belongs to a certain good class of pasting shapes, its vertebrae should be viewed as certain ‘indecomposable’
d
-dimensional subshapes so that taking their union will always recover the original pasting shape. We will make this precise in
3.2
and
3.3
.

Example 3.7
.

Consider the 2-dimensional pasting shape
I
of
3
and its subshapes

I
, but the second is with bounding box given by
((2,1),(4,3))
. Note that in this case,
I
is entire in

is an example of a vertebra. However, the subshape

(0,1)\rightarrow(2,1)\;\;\;\;\;\subset\;\;\;\;\;I

is not an 1-vertebra of
I
since it is not
1-entire:
the 1-box
(0,1)\rightarrow(2,1)
can be obtained by joining the boxes
(0,1)\rightarrow(1,1)
and
(1,1)\rightarrow(2,1)
in
I
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
I
and
J
be pasting shapes. Then we define the
union of
I
and
J
,
I\cup J
, to be the smallest pasting shape that contains both
I
and
J
.
Moreover, we define the
intersection of
I
and
J
,
I\cap J
, to be the largest pasting shape that is contained in
I
and
J
. Concretely,
I\cap J
has boxes given by
B^{d}(I\cap J)=B^{d}(I)\cap B^{d}(J)
.

We have a few important pasting shapes. For natural numbers
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}
, we
define the
d
-dimensional pasting shape

\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]

to be the largest pasting shape whose vertices
are the
d
-tuples contained in
\{0,\dotsc,n_{1}\}\times\dotsb\times\{0,\dotsc,n_{d}\}
, i.e. its boxes are given by

B^{d}(\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}])=\{(x,y)\in B^{d,d}\mid 0\leq x_{a},y_{a}\leq n_{a}\text{ for all $a$}\}.

With the obvious structure maps, these pasting shapes assemble to a
d
-uple cosimplicial object in
\textup{Shape}^{d}

\Delta^{\times d}\rightarrow\textup{Shape}^{d}:(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d})\mapsto\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}].

We will show that this cosimplicial object satisfies the following
co-Segal property
:

Proposition 3.9
.

The structure maps of
\square[\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet]
witness the colimit

\colim_{[k_{a}]\in\mathbb{G}/[n_{a}]}\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{a-1},k_{a},n_{a+1},\dotsc,n_{d}]=\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}].

in
\textup{Shape}^{d}
.

To prove the above proposition, we will make use of the following description
of maps out of
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
:

Proposition 3.10
.

There is a bijection

\textstyle\textup{Shape}^{d}(\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}],I)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{I}(\coprodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}[n_{a}],\mathbb{N}),

natural in
([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])\in\Delta^{\times d}
, where the right-hand side
is the subset

\textstyle\mathrm{Hom}_{I}(\coprodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}[n_{a}],\mathbb{N})\subset\textup{Poset}(\coprodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}[n_{a}],\mathbb{N})=\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}\textup{Poset}([n_{a}],\mathbb{N})

of those tuples
(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d})
which have the property that
each box
(x,y)
with
x_{a},y_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})
for all
a
, is contained in
I
.

Proof.

The key observation is the following. Let
f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I
be a map of pasting shapes, and define

f_{a}(i):=f(0,\dotsc,0,i,0,\dotsc,0)_{a}.

Then we claim that the image of a vertex
x\in\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
is given by

f(x)=(f_{1}(x_{1}),\dotsc,f_{d}(x_{d})).

Indeed, for any index
1\leq a\leq d
, we may consider the projection
p^{a}:=(0,\dotsc,0,x_{a},0,\dotsc,0)
of
x
.
Then
(p^{a},x)
is a
(d-1)
-box of
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
. By the definition of pasting maps, we must then have that

f(p^{a})_{a}=f(x)_{a}
. By definition, we have
f_{a}(x_{a})=f(p^{a})_{a}
so that
f(x)_{a}=f_{a}(x_{a})
.

The correspondence is now given as follows: we carry a map of pasting shapes
f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I
to the tuple
(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d})

defined above. Note that each
f_{a}
is indeed a map of posets
[n_{a}]\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.
There is an inverse to this correspondence: we may carry a tuple
(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{I}(\coprodop\displaylimits[n_{a}],\mathbb{N})
,
to the unique map of pasting shapes
f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I
which is given on vertices by
f(x):=(f_{1}(x_{1}),\dotsc,f_{d}(x_{d})).

∎

Proof of
3
.

Let
I
be a
d
-dimensional pasting shape, and let us write
H_{k}
for the set of tuples

\mathrm{Hom}_{I}([n_{1}]\sqcup\dotsc\sqcup[n_{a-1}]\sqcup[k]\sqcup[n_{a+1}]\sqcup\dotsc\sqcup[n_{d}],\mathbb{N})
. In light of
3
, we have to verify that the canonical map

\textstyle H_{n_{a}}\rightarrow H_{1}\times_{H_{0}}\dotsc\times_{H_{0}}H_{1}

is a bijection. Note that
[n_{a}]
can be written as the iterated pushout

[1]\cup_{[0]}\dotsc\cup_{[0]}[1]

of posets. Hence, the canonical map can be identified with the inclusion

\textstyle H_{n_{a}}\rightarrow H^{\prime}_{n_{a}},

where the subset
H^{\prime}_{n_{a}}\subset\mathrm{Hom}(\coprodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}[n_{a}],\mathbb{N})
contains those tuples
(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d})
which have the following property: if
(x,y)
is
a box so that for each index
b
, there exist indices
i_{b}\leq j_{b}
so that

x_{b}=f_{b}(i_{b}),\quad y_{b}=f_{b}(j_{b}),

and

j_{b}-i_{b}\leq 1
if
b=a
, then
(x,y)
is contained in
I
. One now readily verifies
that
H^{\prime}_{n_{a}}=H_{n_{a}}
by making use of the fact that pasting shapes are closed under joins.
∎

This observation now leads to the following construction:

Construction 3.11
.

Given a
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I
, we define a
d
-uple simplicial set
[I]
, the
nerve of
I
, by setting

[I]_{n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}}:=\textup{Shape}^{d}(\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}],I).

On account of
3
,
[I]
is a
d
-uple Segal set. This is a canonically functorial construction, so that
we obtain a functor

[-]:\textup{Shape}^{d}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{d}(\textup{Set})

that carries pasting shapes to their nerves.
Note that the simplices of the nerve may be described using
3
. In particular,
one can use this description to readily verify that
[-]
is fully faithful.

Corollary 3.12
.

The
d
-uple cosimplicial object
[\square[\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet]]
concides with
\Delta[\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet]
.

Proof.

In light of
3
, we see that

[\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]]_{n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}}\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}\Delta([n_{a}],[m_{a}]),

natural in all variables.
∎

3.1.
Truncations of pasting shapes and grids

In order to define a good class of pasting shapes, we need to introduce the building blocks of
these shapes: so-called
grids
. These are pasting shapes that are in some sense shaped by the standard grids
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Note that this map does not create any new 2-cells, but factors one of the boundary 1-cells. The codomain is in this sense shaped by the domain (modulo boxes of dimension
\leq 1
, they agree), and would be an example of a grid. We want to make this precise and capture this notion.
First, we observe that
d
-uple simplicial sets support a notion of dimension:

Definition 3.13
.

Suppose that
S
is a
d
-uple simplicial set. Then a
d
-uple simplex
\sigma:\Delta[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow S

is called
of dimension
\leq k
if there exists a factorization

\Delta[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow\Delta[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow S

of
\sigma

such that there are at most
k
indices
i
such that
m_{i}\neq 0
.
Moreover, we say that
\sigma
is
of dimension
k
if it is of dimension
\leq k
but not of dimension
\leq(k-1)
.
The
d
-uple simplicial subset of
S
containing those
d
-uple simplices of dimension
\leq k
is called the
k
-truncation of
S
and denoted by

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}S\subset S.

Whenever
S
is the nerve of a pasting shape, the truncation
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}S
can be described as follows.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
I
be a
d
-dimensional pasting shape. Then for
0\leq k\leq d
, we define its
k
-truncation

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}I\subset I
to be the pasting shape with boxes

B^{i}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}I)=\begin{cases}B^{i}(I)&\text{if $i\leq k$},\\
B^{k}(I)&\text{if $i\geq k$}.\end{cases}

We say that
I
is
k-truncated
if
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}I=I
, i.e., whenever every
l
-box of
I
is degenerate for
l>k
.

Example 3.15
.

The following is an important class of examples of
k
-truncated
d
-dimensional pasting shapes. For
d-k
different indices
1\leq a_{1},\dotsc,a_{d-k}\leq d

and integers
c_{1},\dotsc,c_{d-k}
, we can consider the
k
-dimensional hyperplane
H
whose boxes are given by

B^{d}(H)=\{(x,y)\in B^{d,d}\mid x_{a_{i}}=y_{a_{i}}=c_{i}\text{ for all $i=1,\dotsc,d-k$}\}.

We will denote the set of
k
-dimensional hyperplanes by
\mathscr{H}_{k}
.

The following can readily be deduced from the two definitions of truncations that we have seen above:

Proposition 3.16
.

Suppose that
I
is a
d
-dimensional pasting shape, and let
0\leq k\leq d
. Then

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}I=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{H\in\mathscr{H}_{k}}I\cap H,

and

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}[I]=[\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}I]=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{H\in\mathscr{H}_{k}}[I\cap H].

Remark 3.17
.

Suppose that we have a tuple
h\in\mathbb{N}^{\times k}
of indices
1\leq h_{1}<\dotsb<h_{k}\leq d
. Then we obtain a
map of posets
m_{h}:\mathbb{N}^{\times k}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}^{\times d}
so that
m_{h}(x)_{j}=x_{i}
if
j=h_{i}
and
m_{h}(x)_{j}=0
otherwise.
This map gives rise to a fully faithful functor

i_{h}:\textup{Shape}^{k}\rightarrow\textup{Shape}^{d}

that carries a
k
-dimensional pasting shape
I
to the
k
-truncated
d
-dimensional pasting shape whose boxes are given by the image
of the boxes of
I
under
m_{h}\times m_{h}
. The essential image of
i_{h}
is given by those
d
-dimensional pasting shapes that are contained in some hyperplane
H\in\mathcal{H}_{k}

whose associated indices are different from the
h_{i}
’s.
Hence, will implicitly view any
d
-dimensional pasting shape that is contained in some hyperplane
H\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
as a
k
-dimensional pasting shape.
Note that the embedding
i_{h}
is compatible with the nerve functors, in the sense that we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Shape}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Shape}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}^{k}(\textup{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}^{d}(\textup{Set}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{h}^{*}}

where
p_{h}^{*}
is as constructed in
2.1
.

Using the notion of truncations, we can give a description of what it means for a
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I
to shape
a
d
-dimensional pasting shape
J
modulo
(d-1)
-boxes.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
f:I\rightarrow J
be an injective map of
d
-dimensional pasting shapes. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

every non-degenerate
d
-box of
J
is the image of a non-degenerate
d
-box in
I
,

(2)

J
can be written as

J=f(I)\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}J,

(3)

every
d
-dimensional simplex of
[J]
lies in
f([I])
,

(4)

the nerve of
J
can be written as

[J]=f([I])\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[J].

Proof.

We only prove that (1) implies (3). The remaining implications are readily verified. Suppose that we have a
d
-dimensional simplex of
[J]
corresponding to a map

g:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow J,

then we must show that
g
factors through
I
. Since
f
is injective, it induces an isomorphism
f:I\rightarrow f(I)\subset J

to the image
f(I)
of
I
. Hence, we must show that
g
has image in
f(I)
.
We may assume that each map
g_{a}:[n_{a}]\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is injective and
n_{a}\neq 0
. Now, any box
(x,y)
of
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]

lies in the image of an injective map
i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
. Thus it suffices to show that
gi
has image in
f(I
).
But
gi
classifies a non-degenerate
d
-box of
J
,
hence has image in
f(I)
by assumption.
∎

Definition 3.19
.

We call a map of
d
-dimensional pasting shapes
I\rightarrow J

d
-shaping
if it is injective and satisfies any of the equivalent conditions above.

With this terminology now in place, we can introduce the following notion:

Definition 3.20
.

A
d
-dimensional pasting shape
A
is called a
grid
, more specifically, an
open grid
, respectively, a
closed grid
, if
there exists a
d
-shaping map

f:\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A,\text{ resp. }f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A,

with
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}\neq 0
, having the property that any
(d-1)
-box
(x,y)
in
A

satisfies
0\leq x_{a},y_{a}\leq f_{a}(n_{a})
for all indices
a
, and there exists an index
b
such that
x_{b}=y_{b}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{b})
.

Example 3.21
.

The 2-dimensional pasting shape

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,2)}

is an example of a closed grid, and the 2-dimensional pasting shape

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(5,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(5,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(5,2)}

is an open grid.
But note that the following 2-dimensional pasting shape
fails
to be a grid:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,2).}

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
A
be a grid witnessed by a map
f
. Then this map
f
is unique: every other map that witnesses
A
to be a grid coincides with
f
.

Proof.

Note that
A
cannot be open and closed simultaneously. We restrict to the case that
A
is open as the closed case is handled analogously. Let

g:\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow A

be another map that witnesses
A
to be a grid. Similarly to the analysis of
3
, we can identify
f
and
g
respectively
with tuples
(f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d})
,
(g_{1},\dotsc,g_{d})
such that each
(d-1)
-box
(x,y)
with
x_{a},y_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})
, resp.
x_{a},y_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
, is in
A
.
Since
f
and
g
are injective, it suffices to show that
\mathrm{im}(f_{b})=\mathrm{im}(g_{b})
for each index
1\leq b\leq d
. Let
x_{b}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{b})
. Then for
a\neq b
, we may find integers
x_{a}<y_{a}
in
\mathrm{im}(f_{a})
since
n_{a}\neq 0
.
Setting
y_{b}:=x_{b}
, we obtain a non-degenerate
(d-1)
-box
(x,y)
in
A
. Since
g
witnesses
A
to be a grid, we must then have that
x_{b}=y_{b}\in\mathrm{im}(g_{b})
.
Hence
\mathrm{im}(f_{b})\subset\mathrm{im}(g_{b})
. Interchanging the roles of
f
and
g
, we also obtain the reverse inclusion.
∎

The above observation shows that the following definition is well-defined.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
A
be a grid witnessed by a map
f
.
We call the
d
-tuple of points

((f_{1}(0),f_{1}(n_{1})),\dotsc,(f_{d}(0),f_{d}(n_{d})))

the
corners
of
A
.
We define the
boundary

\partial A\subset A

to be the subshape of
A

that consists of the boxes
(x,y)
in
A
such that
x_{a}=y_{a}=f_{a}(0),f_{a}(n_{a})
for some index
a
.
If
n_{1}=\dotsb=n_{d}=1
, then
A
is called a
cell
.

Example 3.24
.

The boundary of the closed grid exhibited in
3.1
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(4,2).}

Example 3.25
.

The pasting shape
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
with
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}\neq 0
is of course a grid. Note that its vertebrae
are given by the cells

V_{(j_{1},\dotsc,j_{d})},\quad 0\leq j_{a}\leq n_{a}-1,

which is the largest
d
-dimensional pasting shape whose vertices
x
satisfy
x_{a}=j_{a},j_{a}+1
for all
a
.

From the definition of grids, it may be readily deduced that the vertebrae of a grid
A
witnessed by a map

f:\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A,\quad\text{or},\quad f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A,

are given by cells whose corners are given by the corners of
f(V_{j})
, for some tuple
j
as above.

3.2.
Admittable and composable pasting shapes

Suppose that
I
is an entire
d
-dimensional pasting shape. Then we would like to show that
I
can be obtained by pasting its vertebrae together. Precisely,
we would like to show that
I
is the union of its vertebrae

I=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{V\subset I\text{ vertebra}}V,

and more importantly, that this colimit is preserved by the nerve functor
[-]:\textup{Shape}^{d}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
. However,
this is not true in general.
For instance, if
d=2
, then we can consider the famous
pinwheel
introduced by Dawson and Paré
[
DP93
]
. This is the 2-uple Segal set associated with the pasting shape

PW:=\hbox to235.89pt{\vbox to123.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 117.94438pt\lower-61.63898pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{{

{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

One can show that mapping out of
[PW]
into a 2-uple Segal space, is not equivalent to mapping out of the nerves of its vertebrae in a compatible fashion.
Already at the level of pasting shapes, the pinwheel cannot be written as the union of its vertebrae. We will demonstrate this
in
3.2
and
3.3
.

Hence, we will need to single out a class of pasting shapes for which such a pasting result holds. The initial objective of this subsection
is to introduce a candidate: the class of
composable
pasting shapes. First, we consider an intermediate, auxiliary class of
admittable
pasting shapes.
Intuitively, these are the pasting shapes that can be built out of grids.
After defining these classes of pasting shapes, will then continue to show that the admittable and composable pasting shapes are considerably better behaved than arbitrary pasting shapes. In particular, we will show that any composable shape can be written as the union of its vertebrae. This can be considered
as an early version of the
pasting theorem
introduced in
3.3
, and similar ideas will be used as input to prove the full strength of the pasting theorem.

Definition 3.26
.

A
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I
is called
admittable
if there
exists a filtration

I_{0}\subset I_{1}\dotsb\subset I_{n}=I,

where
I_{0}
is a grid and
I_{k}=I_{k-1}\cup A_{k}
for a grid
A_{k}\subset I
such that
I_{k-1}\cap A_{k}=\partial A_{k}
is an open vertebra of
I_{k-1}
.
The
corners of
I
are given by the corners of
I_{0}
(it is readily verified that this is independent of the filtration). Moreover, we define
the
boundary of
I

\partial I\subset I

to be the smallest subshape of
I
that contains
all boxes
(x,y)
such that
x_{a}=y_{a}
is equal to a coordinate of the
a
’th corner for some index
a
, or equivalently
\partial I=\partial I_{0}
.

Example 3.27
.

The filtration of 2-dimensional pasting shapes

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

witnesses the rightmost pasting shape to be admittable.

Definition 3.28
.

A
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I
is called
composable
if it meets the following conditions:

(1)

I
is closed admittable (i.e. admittable and closed
d
-entire),

(2)

any closed
k
-entire subshape of
I
, with
k<d
, is admittable when viewed as a
k
-dimensional pasting shape (see
3.1
).

Example 3.29
.

Here are some examples:

•

The 1-dimensional admittable pasting shapes correspond to those subposets of
\mathbb{N}
which are disjoint unions of intervals. The composable ones correspond to the intervals.

•

An example of a 2-dimensional composable pasting shape is the pasting shape

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3,2),}

which classifies one of the relevant composites of the triangle identity in a
2-fold Segal space
(see
5
).

•

A filtration as in
3.2
for the pinwheel
PW
cannot exist
since the only subgrids of the pinwheel are six distinct cells. Hence, the pinwheel is not admittable and, in particular,
not composable.

•

The 2-dimensional pasting shape
I
and the 3-dimensional pasting shape
J
of
3
are composable.

•

All pasting shapes in the filtration of
3.2
are admittable, but only the rightmost shape is closed and composable.

We would now like to study these admittable and composable pasting shapes. In particular, we will show at the end of this subsection the following theorem which may be considered
as a stepping stone to
3.49
, the general pasting theorem:

Theorem 3.30
.

Let
I
be a composable pasting shape. Then each vertebra of
I
is composable, and
I
can be written as the union of its vertebrae.

In order to prove structural properties of admittable pasting shapes (it is also used in the proof of the general pasting theorem), the following notion of
division pairs
turns out to be useful. It is precisely the datum
of a stage in a filtration that appears in
3.2
:

Definition 3.31
.

A
division pair
for an admittable pasting shape
I
is a tuple
(K,J)
of admittable subshapes of
I
such that
K\cap J=\partial J
is an open vertebra of
K
, and
I=K\cup J
.

.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Proposition 3.33
.

Let
I
be an admittable pasting shape. Then the following is true:

(1)

For any open vertebra
V
of
I
with corners
((\alpha_{1},\omega_{1}),\dotsc,(\alpha_{d},\omega_{d}))
, every box
(x,y)
in
I

has
y_{a}\leq\alpha_{a}
or
x_{a}\geq\omega_{a}
for some index
a
.

(2)

If
(K,J)
is a division pair for an admittable pasting shape
I
, where
J
has corners given by
((\alpha_{1},\omega_{1}),\dotsc,(\alpha_{d},\omega_{d}))
,
then
J
is entire, and for any box
(x,y)
in
I
we have that

(x,y)
is in
K
if and only if there exists an index
a
such that
y_{a}\leq\alpha_{a}
or
x_{a}\geq\omega_{a}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(K,J)
is a division pair for an admittable pasting shape
I
such that (1) holds for
K
and
J
.
Denote the corners of
J
by
((\alpha_{1},\omega_{1}),\dotsc,(\alpha_{d},\omega_{d}))
.
We inductively define a subset
N^{k}
of non-degenerate
k
-boxes of
I
as follows. We set
N^{0}:=B^{0}(K)\cup B^{0}(J)
. If
k\geq 1
and
N^{k-1}

is defined, then we define
N^{k}
to be the set of non-degenerate
k
-boxes
(x,y)
in
I
such that the faces
of
(x,y)
are in
N^{k-1}
and one of the following properties is met:

(i)

(x,y)\in B^{k}(K)\cup B^{k}(J)
,

(ii)

for all indices
a
, we have
x_{a}<\omega_{a}
and
y_{a}>\alpha_{a}
, and there exists an index
b
so that
x_{b}<\alpha_{b}
or

y_{b}>\omega_{b}
.

Here, condition (ii) will capture boxes that are obtained by joining boxes in
K
with boxes in
J
which do not lie in
K\cap J=\partial J
. Note that a non-degenerate
k
-box
cannot have properties (i) and (ii) simultaneously.

One now readily checks that the boxes in the subset

\bigcupop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{d}N^{k}\subset B^{d}(I)

are closed under faces and joins (by induction on
k
), and hence, constitute a subshape
I^{\prime}\subset I
.
By construction,
K
and
J
are contained in
I^{\prime}
. Since
I=K\cup J
, we must have that
I=I^{\prime}
. Thus the non-degenerate
k
-boxes
of
I
are given by
N^{k}
. From this description and the fact that (1) holds for
K
, it can be deduced
that (2) holds. Moreover, using that (1) also holds for
J
, we can now deduce that (1) holds for
I
as well.
Hence, we may reduce the proof of the statement to showing that (1) holds any grid, which is clear.
∎

In the demonstration of
3.2
above, we have used division pairs to induct our way up to each admittable pasting shape. Another convenient
way of giving similarly flavored inductive proofs is using the notion of
heights
:

Definition 3.34
.

A
decomposition
of an admittable pasting shape
I
is a pair
(A,\{J_{i}\})
, where
A\subset I
is an open subgrid of
I
,
\{J_{i}\}
is a collection
of admittable subshapes of
I
, such the intersections
A\cap J_{i}=\partial J_{i}
are all mutually distinct open vertebrae of
A
, and

I
can be written as

I=A\cup\bigcupop\displaylimits J_{i}.

We say that an admittable pasting shape
I
has
height
0 if it is a cell. Inductively, we say that a pasting shape has
height
h
if
there exists a decomposition
(A,\{J_{i}\})
of
I
where each
J_{i}
height
h-1
.

The proof of the following proposition shows how one can obtain a decomposition for an admittable pasting shape:

Proposition 3.36
.

Any admittable pasting shape admits a decomposition and has a height.

Proof.

Suppose that
I
is an admittable pasting shape with a filtration as in
3.2
.
Then for
1\leq k\leq n
, we consider the pair

D_{k}=(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}I_{0},S_{k}),

where
S_{k}
is the set of entire subshapes of
I_{k}
whose bounding boxes are given by the corners of vertebrae of
I_{0}
. We
show by induction that
D_{k}
is a decomposition for
I_{k}
. For
k=0
, this is clear.
Suppose that
D_{k-1}
is a decomposition for
I_{k-1}
. Now,
I_{k}=I_{k-1}\cup A_{k}
, where
A_{k}
is a grid and
A_{k}\cap I_{k-1}=\partial A_{k}
is a vertebra of
I_{k-1}
.
3.2
implies
that
\partial A_{k}\subset E
of some subshape
E\in S_{k-1}
. Let
E^{\prime}
be the entire subshape in
S_{k}
with the same bounding box.
Then
E^{\prime}=(I_{k-1}\cup A_{k})\cap E^{\prime}=E\cup A_{k}
and
E\cap A_{k}=\partial A_{k}
must be a vertebra of
E
. Thus
E^{\prime}
is again admittable. Moreover, we see that
S_{k}=S_{k-1}\setminus\{E\}\cup\{E^{\prime}\}
.
All in all, this implies that
D_{k}
is a decomposition for
I_{k}
. It is readily verified that
I
has a height.
∎

Remark 3.37
.

Note that the height of an admittable pasting shape
I
is not unique. If
I
has height
h
then
I
has all heights greater than
h
as well.

Example 3.38
.

The 2-dimensional admittable pasting shape
I
of
3
has (minimal) height
3
.

The following
factorization property
is an important property of admittable pasting shapes:

Lemma 3.39
.

Suppose that
I
is an admittable pasting shape with decomposition
(A,\{J_{i}\})
, where the open grid
A
is witnessed by the map

f:\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A.

Then for any box
(x,y)\in I
, we have
(x,y)\in A
if and only if
x_{b}=y_{b}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{b})
for some index
b
. If
(x,y)\notin A
, then there exists an injective map

g:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I,

such that

\mathrm{im}(g_{a})=\{t_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\mid x_{a}\leq t_{a}\leq y_{a}\}\cup\{x_{a},y_{a}\}.

In particular, it follows that any injective map

h:\square[p_{1},\dotsc,p_{d}]\rightarrow I

with
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{d}\neq 0
, admits an extension

h^{\prime}:\square[p_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,p_{d}^{\prime}]\rightarrow I

such that
\mathrm{im}(h^{\prime}_{a})=\{t_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\mid h_{a}(0)\leq t_{a}\leq h_{a}(p_{a})\}\cup\mathrm{im}(h_{a})
.

Proof.

Similarly as in the proof of
3.2
, we inductively define sets
N^{k}
of non-degenerate
k
-boxes of
I
.
We set
N^{0}:=B^{0}(A)\cup\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}B^{0}(J_{i})
. If
N^{k-1}
is defined, we define
N^{k}
to be the set
of non-degenerate
k
-boxes
(x,y)
of
I
whose faces are in
N^{k-1}
, and which have one of the following properties:

(1)

(x,y)\in B^{k}(A)
,

(2)

(x,y)\notin B^{k}(A)
,
x_{a},y_{a}\notin\mathrm{im}(f_{a})
if
x_{a}=y_{a}
, and there exists an injective map

g:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I,

such that

\mathrm{im}(g_{a})=\{t_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\mid x_{a}\leq t_{a}\leq y_{a}\}\cup\{x_{a},y_{a}\}.

By construction, the subset of boxes

\bigcupop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{d}N^{k}\subset B^{d}(I)

is closed under faces. We will show that it is also closed under joins and hence defines a subshape
I^{\prime}\subset I
.

Clearly,
N^{0}
is closed under joins. Suppose that
N^{k-1}
is closed under joins. Then we show
that the join of adjacent boxes
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
in
N^{k}
is again in
N^{k}
. The faces of the join
(x,y^{\prime})
must be in
N^{k-1}
, since
N^{k-1}
is assumed to be closed under joins.
Thus we must check that the join
(x,y^{\prime})
has property (1) or (2).
If
k=d
,
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
must have property (2) since
A
has no non-degenerate
d
-boxes. If
k<d
, then
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
either must have both property (1) or both property (2),
since any box
(s,t)
in
A
has the property that
s_{b}=t_{b}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{b})
for some index
b
.
In both cases, it is readily verified that the join of
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
again has the same property, hence is in
N^{k}
.

We now claim that the resulting subshape
I^{\prime}
coincides with
I
. We then obtain a description of the boxes of
I
, from which the first statement of the lemma
follows.
By assumption, we may write
I
as the union

I=A\cup J_{1}\cup\dotsb\cup J_{m},

Thus it suffices that
A
and the
J_{i}
’s are contained in
I^{\prime}
. The first assertion is clear.
Suppose that
(x,y)
is a box in
J_{i}
, then clearly the desired injective map
g
exists (with
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}\leq 1)
.
If
x_{b}=y_{b}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{b})
for some index
b
, then we must have that
(x,y)
is contained in the boundary
\partial J_{i}\subset A
. Thus, in either case, we have

(x,y)\in I^{\prime}
.

The latter statement of the lemma follows from the preceding assertion and the fact that
A
has no non-degenerate
d
-boxes.
∎

and the open subgrid
A\subset I

. This open subgrid

(A,\{J_{i}\})
for

.
