\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;\;\;\;\;\subset\;\;\;\;\;I.

Proposition 3.41
.

Any closed entire subshape
E
of a
d
-dimensional admittable pasting shape
I
is again admittable.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the height
h
of
I
. If
h=0
, the statement is clear. Suppose that the statement holds
for any admittable pasting shape of height
h
.

Let
I
be a composable pasting shape of height
h+1
with decomposition
(A,\{J_{i}\})
. Write
f:\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A

for the map that witnesses
A
to be an open grid. Denote the bounding box of
E
by
(x,y)
. Since
E
is entire,
(x,y)
must be a non-degenerate
d
-box contained in
I
.
Consequently, there exists an extension

g:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I,

with
\mathrm{im}(g_{a})=\{t_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\mid x_{a}\leq t_{a}\leq y_{a}\}\cup\{x_{a},y_{a}\}
on account of
3.2
.
Let
B
be the open subgrid of
I
determined by
g
. We note that for any inert map

j:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}],

the restriction
gj
classifies the corners of a closed entire,
d
-dimensional subshape
E_{j}\subset E
, which has the property that
E\subset J_{i}
for some
i
.
Thus
E_{j}
is admittable by the induction hypothesis. Note that
E_{j}\cap B=\partial E_{j}
. Using
3.2
, one may again show that

E=B\cup\bigcupop\displaylimits_{j}E_{j}.

From this it follows that
E
is admittable with decomposition
(B,\{E_{j}\})
.
∎

Using a similar strategy as employed in the proof of
3.2
, we can show a stronger factorization property for composable pasting shapes:

Lemma 3.42
.

Suppose that
I
is a
d
-dimensional composable pasting shape and let

f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I

be an injective map that carries corners to corners. Then for any box
(x,y)\in I
, there exists an injective map

g:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I,

such that

\mathrm{im}(g_{a})=\{t_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\mid x_{a}\leq t_{a}\leq y_{a}\}\cup\{x_{a},y_{a}\}.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the dimension
d
. For
d=0
, the statement is clear. Suppose the statement holds for
(d-1)
-dimensional composable pasting shapes.
If
I
has height
0
, then
I
is a cell, and there is nothing to prove. Suppose that the statement holds for all
d
-dimensional composable pasting shapes
that have height
h
.

Let
I
be a composable pasting shape of height
h+1
with decomposition
(A,\{J_{i}\})
. During this proof, we write
N^{k}
for the set of non-degenerate
k
-boxes
(x,y)
of
I
with the property that there exists an injective map

g:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I,

such that

\mathrm{im}(g_{a})=\{t_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\mid x_{a}\leq t_{a}\leq y_{a}\}\cup\{x_{a},y_{a}\}.

Then the subset of boxes given by

\bigcupop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{d}N^{k}\subset B^{d}(I)

is closed under joins and faces, hence constitutes a subshape
I^{\prime}\subset I
. It now suffices to show that
I^{\prime}
contains
J_{1},\dotsc,J_{n}
and
A
.

Suppose that
(x,y)
is a box of
A
. Then there exists an index
b
so that
x_{b}=y_{b}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{b})
. The map

f
restricts to a
(d-1)
-truncated map

f^{\prime}:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{b-1},0,n_{b+1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A\cap H.

This can be viewed as a map between
(d-1)
-dimensional pasting shapes. As the slice
A\cap H
must be
(d-1)
-dimensional composable pasting shape, for which the statement already holds,
it can now readily be deduced that
(x,y)\in I^{\prime}
.

It remains to show that each box
(x,y)
of
J_{i}
is contained in
I^{\prime}
. We will write

\phi:\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}\square[p_{1},\dotsc,p_{d}]\rightarrow A,

for the injective map that witnesses
A
to be an open grid. On account of
3.2
,
there exists an injective map

f^{\prime}:\square[n_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,n_{d}^{\prime}]\rightarrow I

such that
\mathrm{im}(f^{\prime}_{a})=\mathrm{im}(\phi_{a})\cup\mathrm{im}(f_{a})
. Note that the corners of
J_{i}
are classified by an inert map

j:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[p_{1},\dotsc,p_{d}],

which determines a restriction
r
of
f^{\prime}
that has image given by

\mathrm{im}(r_{a})=\{t_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\mid j_{a}(0)\leq t_{a}\leq j_{a}(1)\}\cup\{j_{a}(0),j_{a}(1)\}\subset\mathrm{im}(f^{\prime}_{a})

for all
a
. The lemma holds for
J_{i}
by assumption as it is composable and has height
h
, hence
we may apply the lemma with respect to
(x,y)
and
r
, yielding the desired factorization
g
for
(x,y)
.
∎

Proof of
3.30
.

We proceed by induction on the height
h
of
I
. If

I
has height
0
, there is nothing to show. Suppose now that the theorem holds for any
d
-dimensional composable pasting shape
I
of height
h
.
Let
I
be a composable pasting shape of height
h+1
with decomposition
(A,\{J_{i}\})
. Then there exists an injective map

f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I

such that
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}f
witnesses
A
to be an open grid. Using
3.2
and
3.2
,
one may verify that the vertebrae of
I
are given by the vertebrae of the
J_{i}
’s. Thus the
induction hypothesis asserts that the vertebrae of

I
are composable and

\bigcupop\displaylimits_{V\subset I\textit{ vertebra }}V=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}J_{i}.

Hence, we must show every box
(x,y)
of
I
is contained in the union of the
J_{i}
’s.
This readily follows from the factorization property of
3.2
.
∎

We may slightly strengthen
3.30
by introducing so-called
locally composable
pasting shapes. This is
an auxiliary notion, albeit a pragmatic notion to set up the theory.

Definition 3.43
.

A
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I
with finitely many boxes is called
locally composable
if every closed
k
-entire subshape of
I
, with
1\leq k\leq d
, is a
k
-dimensional admittable pasting shape.

The following is a direct result of
3.2
:

Proposition 3.44
.

A
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I
is composable if and only if
I
is locally composable and closed
d
-entire.

Corollary 3.45
.

Suppose that
I
is a
d
-dimensional locally composable pasting shape, then
I
can be written as the union

I=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{V\subset I\text{ closed k-vertebra, }0\leq k\leq d}V.

Proof.

Suppose that
(x,y)
is a
k
-box of
I
. Then
(x,y)
determines a
k
-entire subshape
E
of
I
which is a
k
-dimensional composable pasting shape by assumption. In view of
3.30
,

E
may be written as the union of its (necessarily closed)
k
-vertebrae. Hence
(x,y)
is contained in the union that is displayed in the statement of the corollary.
∎

Example 3.46
.

Note that
3.30
fails to be true in the setting of non-composable pasting shapes. Consider the pinwheel
PW
that was defined
at the start of
3.2
. Then there is a subshape

PW^{\circ}\subset PW

whose boxes are given by
B^{2}(PW^{\circ})=B^{2}(PW)\setminus\{((0,0),(3,3))\}
. It is readily verified
that
PW^{\circ}
is locally composable. Since every
1
-vertebra of
PW^{\circ}
is contained in a
2
-vertebra of
PW^{\circ}
,

3.2
asserts that

PW^{\circ}=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{V\subset PW^{\circ}\text{ $k$-vertebra, }0\leq k\leq 2}V=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{V\subset PW^{\circ}\text{ vertebra}}V.

But the vertebrae of
PW^{\circ}
are precisely those of
PW
. Since
PW\neq PW^{\circ}
, this shows that
3.30
fails for the pinwheel.

3.3.
The pasting theorem

Having developed the basic theory of pasting shapes, we are now able to formulate the pasting theorem which will be proven in the next section.

Definition 3.47
.

Let
I
be a
d
-dimensional pasting shape. Then a
covering of
I
is a collection
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}
of subshapes of
I
, such that

(1)

every closed
k
-entire subshape of
I_{i}
, with
0\leq k\leq d
, is entire in
I
,

(2)

every closed
k
-vertebra of
I
, with
0\leq k\leq d
, is contained in some
I_{i}
.

Example 3.48
.

We have the following canonical examples of coverings:

(1)

Every composable pasting shape is covered by its vertebrae.

(2)

In general, if
I
is a locally composable pasting shape, then the collection of closed
k
-vertebrae of
I
, where
k
ranges over all dimensions
0,\dotsc,d
,
is a covering of
I
.

(3)

If
I
is an admittable pasting shape with decomposition
(A,\{J_{i}\})
then
A
together with the
J_{i}
’s form a covering for
I
.

We would like to phrase our pasting theorem for
d
-uple Segal spaces
as a statement about the nerve functor preserving certain colimits.
To this end, we will need to understand how unions of pasting shapes may be described as colimits.
This can be done as follows. Suppose that
S
is a set of
d
-dimensional pasting shapes. Then we may consider the set of pasting shapes given by the non-empty intersections

\textstyle\mathcal{P}(S):=\{\bigcapop\displaylimits_{I\in T}I\mid T\subset S\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}

which becomes a (finite) poset under inclusion. Now, one may readily verify that the colimit
of the tautological functor

Q:\mathcal{P}(S)\rightarrow\textup{Shape}^{d}:J\mapsto J

is given by
the union
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{I\in S}I
.

Theorem 3.49

(The pasting theorem)
.

Suppose that
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}
is a covering of a
d
-dimensional locally composable pasting shape
I
. Then
I
can be written as

I=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}I_{i},

and this union is preserved by the nerve functor
[-]:\textup{Shape}^{d}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
, so that the comparison map

\textstyle\colim_{J\in\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})}[J]\rightarrow[I]

is an equivalence of
d
-uple Segal spaces.

Remark 3.50
.

The pasting theorem can also be rephrased as follows. Suppose that
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}
is a covering of a locally composable pasting shape
I
.
Since
\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
is a reflective subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S})
, we may compute and reflect the colimit appearing in the statement in the latter category
so that the statement of the theorem is equivalent to saying that the map

\textstyle\colim_{J\in\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})}[J]\rightarrow[I]

of
d
-uple simplicial spaces is a Segal equivalence.

More is true: the colimit on the left may be computed in
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\textup{Set})
. The colimit is then given by the ordinary union so that the theorem equivalently asserts that the inclusion

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}[I_{i}]\rightarrow[I]

of
d
-uple simplicial sets is a Segal equivalence. To deduce that we can compute the colimit in
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\textup{Set})
, we can proceed level-wise and apply similar
reasoning as in the proof of
2.2
. We have to show that the homotopy colimit (w.r.t. the Kan-Quillen model structure on
\mathrm{sSet}
) of the diagram

\textstyle[Q]_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}}:\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})\rightarrow\mathrm{sSet}:J\mapsto[J]_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}}.

is given by the ordinary colimit for all
k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}\geq 0
.

We will do so by making use of the theory of
Reedy model structures
(see
[
Hir03
, Section 15.1]
or
[
RV14
]
).
Recall that
\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})
is a finite poset, so it comes with a canonical Reedy structure (see
[
RV14
, Example 2.3]
). So the homotopy colimit of a general
diagram
F:\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})\rightarrow\mathrm{sSet}
may be computed as the colimit of
F

if
F
is Reedy cofibrant.
By definition, the diagram
F
is Reedy cofibrant if and only if for any the latching map for

F
at each
J\in\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})

\textstyle\colim_{J^{\prime}\subsetneq J\in\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})}F(J^{\prime})\rightarrow F(J).

is a monomorphism of simplicial sets. In the case that
F=[Q]_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}}
, the latching map at such
J
is given by the canonical map

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{J^{\prime}\subsetneq J\in\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})}[J^{\prime}]_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}}\rightarrow[J]_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}},

which is a monomorphism.

If
I
is a composable pasting shape, then the vertebrae of
I
give a canonical covering of
I
to which the above theorem applies.

Definition 3.51
.

Let
I
be a
d
-dimensional composable pasting shape.
The
spine
of
[I]
is the
d
-uple simplicial subset of
[I]
given by the union

\mathrm{Sp}[I]:=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{V\subset I\textit{ vertebra}}[V]\subset[I].

We will call the resulting inclusion
\mathrm{Sp}[I]\rightarrow[I]
the
spine inclusion for
I
.

Corollary 3.52
.

The spine inclusion for a composable pasting shape
I

is a Segal equivalence.

Remark 3.53
.

The spine inclusions defined above generalize the spine inclusions that were introduced in
2.1
.
Consider the grid
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
so that
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}\neq 0
. We have
exhibited the collection
\mathcal{U}
of vertebrae of such a standard grid in
3.1
.
There is a canonical isomorphism of categories

\mathbb{G}^{\times d}/([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{U})

that carries a tuple
(i_{a}:[k_{a}]\rightarrow[n_{a}])
to the image of
the corresponding map
i:\square[k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d}]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
of pasting shapes.
Using the identifications of
3
, we obtain a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim_{([k_{1}],\dotsc,[k_{d}])\in\mathbb{G}^{\times d}/([n_{1}],\dotsc,[n_{d}])}\Delta[k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim_{J\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{U})}[J]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

in
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S})
. The right arrow is precisely the spine inclusion for the grid
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
on account of
3.3
. Thus the spine inclusion for the standard grid is a Segal equivalence by definition.

Example 3.54
.

Using a similar argument as in
3.2
, one sees that the spine inclusion for
PW
is not a Segal equivalence.
Namely, it factors as

\mathrm{Sp}[PW]=\mathrm{Sp}[PW^{\circ}]\rightarrow[PW^{\circ}]\rightarrow[PW].

The pasting theorem asserts that the first inclusion is a Segal equivalence. By the 2-out-of-3 principle, the spine inclusion
for
PW
is a Segal equivalence precisely when the inclusion
[PW^{\circ}]\rightarrow[PW]
is a Segal equivalence. But this
cannot be the case since the nerve functor is fully faithful and
PW\neq PW^{\circ}
.

Corollary 3.55
.

Consider a
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I
and a
d
-uple Segal space
X
.
Suppose that
I
is a composable shape and that we have a family of maps

f_{V}:[V]\rightarrow X,\quad V\subset I\text{ vertebra},

which
are compatible in the sense that
it defines a map
\mathrm{Sp}[I]\rightarrow X

of
d
-uple simplicial spaces. Then the space
C
of composites defined by the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})}([I],X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(f_{V})\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times d},\mathscr{S})}(\mathrm{Sp}[I],X),}

is contractible.

4.
Proof of the pasting theorem

Our strategy for proving the pasting theorem is by proceeding inductively on the dimension of the pasting shapes. We introduce the following auxiliary notion:

Definition 4.1
.

We call a
d
-dimensional pasting shape
I

good
if the map

\mathrm{Sp}[I]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]\rightarrow[I]

is a Segal equivalence.

Remark 4.2
.

We may describe the
d
-uple simplicial subset of
[I]
appearing in the domain of the map above, alternatively by

\mathrm{Sp}[I]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow I\text{ injective},\\
\mathrm{im}(i)\text{ is contained in a (closed) vertebra of $I$}\end{subarray}}i[\square[1,\dotsc,1]]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I].

The following theorem is the crucial ingredient that makes the induction work:

Theorem 4.3
.

Every
d
-dimensional admittable pasting shape is good.

The proof of this theorem is deferred to the end of this section. Instead, we will now demonstrate how the pasting theorem follows from this fact. We distill two
technical steps in this demonstration as lemmas. They will also be of use later.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose that
I
is a
d
-dimensional pasting shape so that any closed entire subshape of
I

is good.
If
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}
is a collection
of subshapes of
I

with the property that any closed entire subshape of
I_{i}
is an entire subshape of
I
, then the inclusion

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\mathrm{Sp}[I_{i}]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I_{i}]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}[I_{i}]

of
d
-uple Segal sets is a Segal equivalence.

Proof.

During this proof, we will call a subshape
J
of
I

total
if it has the property that any closed entire subshape of
J
is again an entire subshape of
I
.
We consider the set
S
of pairs of
d
-uple simplicial subsets of
[I]

(\mathrm{Sp}[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[J],[J])

where
J
is
(d-1)
-truncated or a total subshape of
I
. One readily verifies that
S
meets condition (1) of
2.2
,
so that we can reduce to showing that the inclusion

i:\mathrm{Sp}[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[J]\rightarrow[J],

is a Segal equivalence for
J

(d-1)
-truncated or total. If
J
is
(d-1)
-truncated, then this inclusion is the identity.
Hence, we may assume that
J
is total.
Let
E_{1},\dotsc,E_{m}
be all closed entire subshapes of
J
. Then the inclusion
i
is precisely the inclusion

\textstyle i:\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{Sp}[E_{i}]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}[E_{i}]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I].

This inclusion is induced by the map

\textstyle j:\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{Sp}[E_{i}]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[E_{i}]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}[E_{i}]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[E_{i}]

under taking pushouts along the inclusion
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[E_{i}]\rightarrow\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]
, hence it suffices to show that

j
is a Segal equivalence.
We can make use of
2.2
once again to verify this. To this end, consider the subset
S^{\prime}\subset S
which
now contains the pairs associated to
(d-1)
-truncated or closed entire subshapes of
I
. Then
S^{\prime}
again meets
condition (1) of
2.2
. By assumption,
S^{\prime}
also meets (2), so that it follows that
j
is a Segal equivalence.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Fix an integer
0\leq d^{\prime}\leq d
.
Suppose that
I
is a
d
-dimensional pasting shape that has the property that any closed
k
-entire subshape of
I
with
k\leq d^{\prime}
, viewed as a
k
-dimensional pasting shape,
is good.
If
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}
is a cover of
I
, then the inclusion

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}[I_{i}]\rightarrow\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}[I]

of
d
-uple Segal sets is a Segal equivalence for every
0\leq k\leq d^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Throughout, let us write

\textstyle j:\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}[I_{i}]\rightarrow[I]

for the inclusion.
We will show that each truncated inclusion
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}j
is a Segal equivalence by induction on
0\leq k\leq d^{\prime}
. For
k=0
, the statement is clear. Suppose that the statement holds for
k-1<d^{\prime}
.
Then we may consider the factorization

\textstyle\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}[I_{i}]\rightarrow\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}[I_{i}]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[I]\xrightarrow{j_{k}}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}[I]

of the truncated inclusion
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}j
. Note that the map
on the left is a pushout of the inclusion map
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}j
, which is a Segal equivalence by assumption.
Hence, this map is again a Segal equivalence.
In light of the 2-out-of-3 principle, the truncation
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k}j
is a Segal equivalence precisely if the inclusion
j_{k}
is a Segal equivalence.

In order to prove that
j_{k}
is a Segal equivalence, we will exploit
3.1
which asserts that
j_{k}
can be written as a union of inclusions

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{H\in\mathscr{H}_{k}}\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}{[I_{i}\cap H]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[I]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{H\in\mathscr{H}_{k}}[I\cap H].

Hence,
2.2
implies that it suffices to verify that for any
H\in\mathscr{H}_{k}
, the inclusion

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}{[I_{i}\cap H]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[I]\rightarrow[I\cap H]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[I]

is an equivalence.
In turn, this map is induced by the map

\textstyle j_{k}^{H}:\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}{[I_{i}\cap H]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[I\cap H]\rightarrow[I\cap H],

under taking pushouts along
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[I\cap H]\rightarrow\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[I]
, so that
it is enough to demonstrate that
j_{k}^{H}
is an equivalence.

To this end, we note that the slices
I_{0}\cap H,\dotsc,I_{n}\cap H\subset I\cap H
correspond to
k
-dimensional pasting shapes
J_{0},\dotsc,J_{n}\subset J

under the embedding
i_{h}
of
3.1
for a suitable choice of
k
-tuple
h
. It readily follows from the definitions that
J_{0},\dotsc,J_{n}
again form a covering for

J
. The compatibility of
i_{h}
with the nerve functor (see
3.1
),
allows us to identify
j_{k}^{H}
with the image of the inclusion

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}{[J_{i}]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[J]\rightarrow[J]

of
k
-uple simplicial sets under the functor
p_{h}^{*}
(defined in
2.1
).
The right adjoint of
p_{h}^{*}
preserves iterated Segal spaces on account of
2.1
.
Hence, the functor
p_{h}^{*}
will preserve Segal equivalences. Thus it suffices to show that
the above inclusion of
k
-uple simplicial sets is a Segal equivalence.
This fits in a commutative square of inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}{\mathrm{Sp}[J_{i}]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[J]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}{[J_{i}]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[J]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[J]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[J]}.}

The left vertical map must be the identity because
J_{0},\dotsc,J_{n}
cover
J
. Moreover,
4.3
implies that the bottom map is a Segal equivalence.
The top map is induced by the inclusion

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}{\mathrm{Sp}[J_{i}]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[J_{i}]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}{[J_{i}]}

under taking pushouts along the map
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq k-1}[J_{i}]\rightarrow[J]
. In light of our assumptions,
this inclusion will be an equivalence by
4
,
so the desired result now follows from the 2-out-of-3 principle.
∎

We are now ready to give a proof of
3.49
assuming that
4.3
holds:

Proof of
3.49
.

The fact that
I
decomposes as
I=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}I_{i}
follows directly from
3.2
and the definition of a covering. One can retrieve
the main result
from the observations in
3.3
and
4
.
∎

4.1.
Prototypical good pasting shapes

It remains to prove
4.3
. We will exhibit a few prototypical examples of good pasting shapes.

Proposition 4.6
.

The standard grids
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
are good.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the dimension
d
. If
d=0
, then there is nothing to show. Suppose that the proposition
holds for the
k
-dimensional standard grids with
k\leq d-1
. Consider a
d
-dimensional standard grid
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
. If
n_{a}=0
for some index
a
, there is nothing to show.
Hence, we may assume that
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}\neq 0
. In what follows, we will suppress the notation of this tuple for brevity. The spine inclusion of
\square
factors as

\mathrm{Sp}[\square]\xrightarrow{j}\mathrm{Sp}[\square]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[\square]\rightarrow[\square].

We have already demonstrated in
3.3
that the composite is a Segal equivalence. Each closed
k
-entire subshape of
\square

is isomorphic to a
k
-dimensional standard grid, hence it is good for
k\leq d-1
on account of the induction hypothesis.
An application of
4
with respect to the covering given by the vertebrae of
\square

now gives that the truncated inclusion

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}\mathrm{Sp}[\square]\rightarrow\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[\square]

is a Segal equivalence. As
j
is a pushout of this map, this proves the statement in light of the 2-out-of-3 principle.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

Any grid is good.

Proof.

Let
A
be a
d
-dimensional grid. If
A
is open, then
4
implies that there is nothing to show. Hence, we may assume that
there exists a map

f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A,

with
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}\neq 0
, that witnesses
A
to be a grid.
When we combine the description given in
4
, the description of the vertebrae of
A

in
3.1
, and the fact that
f
is
d
-shaping, we deduce that we have a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[\square]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[\square]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[A]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[A]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\square]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A]}.}

The left map is a Segal equivalence on account of
4.1
, hence the result follows.
∎

The following is also an important example of a good pasting shape:

Definition 4.8
.

Suppose that

i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]

is an injective map of
d
-dimensional pasting shapes.
Then we define the (admittable) pasting shape

\boxdot[i]\subset\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]

to be the subshape of
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
that consists of all those boxes
(x,y)
such that
there exists an index
a
with
x_{a}\leq y_{a}\leq i_{a}(0)
or
i_{a}(1)\leq x_{a}\leq y_{a}
(one readily checks that these boxes are closed
under faces and joins).

Example 4.9
.

The following is a picture for
\boxdot[i]
, with maps
i_{1}:[1]\rightarrow[6],i_{2}:[1]\rightarrow[4]
starting and ending at
2,5
and
1,3
respectively:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

Proposition 4.10
.

Suppose that
i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
is an injective map, then the
pasting shape
\boxdot[i]
is good.

Let us first make the necessary preparations for its proof.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
be an injective map.
For a sign
\sigma\in\{-,+\}
and index
1\leq a\leq d
, we define

M^{\sigma}_{a}[i]\subset\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]

to be the subshape that
contains all boxes whose vertices
x
satisfy

\begin{cases}x_{a}\leq i_{a}(0)&\text{if $\sigma=-$},\\
x_{a}\geq i_{a}(1)&\text{if $\sigma=+$}.\end{cases}

Note that
M^{\sigma}_{a}[i]
is contained in
\boxdot[i]
.
Moreover, we define

A[i]\subset\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]

to be to be the largest subshape of
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
whose vertices
x
satisfy
i_{a}(0)\leq x_{a}\leq i_{a}(1)
for all indices
a
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
be an injective map.
Then any map of pasting shapes
\square[k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d}]\rightarrow\boxdot[i]
factors through one of
the subshapes
M^{\sigma}_{a}[i]
.

Proof.

Consider a map of pasting shapes
f:\square[k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d}]\rightarrow\boxdot[i]
and suppose to the contrary that
f
does not factor through a single

M^{\sigma}_{a}[i]
. Then for any
a
, there exist a
t_{a}
and
t^{\prime}_{a}
in the image of
f_{a}
such that
t_{a}>i_{a}(0)
and
t^{\prime}_{a}<i_{a}(1)
. Define
x_{a}:=\min(t_{a},t^{\prime}_{a})
and
y_{a}:=\max(t_{a},t^{\prime}_{a})
.
Then
(x,y)
is in the image of
f
, and hence must be a box in
\boxdot[i]
. Thus there is an index
a
such that
x_{a}\leq y_{a}\leq i_{a}(0)
or
i_{a}(1)\leq x_{a}\leq y_{a}
.
This is a contradiction as
i_{a}(1)>t^{\prime}_{a}\geq x_{a}
and
y_{a}\geq t_{a}>i_{a}(0)
.
∎

Proof of
4.1
.

For brevity, we drop the notation of the map
i
in what follows.
Note that
4.1
precisely asserts that the inclusion

\mathrm{Sp}[\boxdot]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[\boxdot]\rightarrow[\boxdot]

is given by

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{\sigma\in\{+,-\},1\leq a\leq d}\mathrm{Sp}[M^{\sigma}_{a}]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[M^{\sigma}_{a}]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{\sigma\in\{+,-\},1\leq a\leq d}[M^{\sigma}_{a}].

We may apply
4
to show that this is a Segal equivalence. To this end, we must show that
any closed entire subshape
E
of
\boxdot
is good.
Indeed,
4.1
implies that
E
must be contained in some
M^{\sigma}_{a}
. In this case,
M^{\sigma}_{a}
is (isomorphic to) a standard grid,
and hence
E
is a standard grid as well. Thus
E
is good on account of
4.1
.
∎

4.2.
Fillable shapes

Until now we have dealt with concrete admittable pasting shapes and shown that they are good. In order to deal with arbitrary
admittable pasting shapes and show that they are good as well, we need some machinery to bootstrap the results we have obtained so far. We will
develop this in the following subsection.

Definition 4.13
.

A division pair
(K,J)
for an admittable pasting shape
I
is called
good
if
K
and
J
are both good.

The following is a result of the main theorem,
4.20
, of this final subsection:

Corollary 4.14
.

Suppose that
(K,J)
is a good division pair for
I
so that the corners of
J
are given by
(\alpha_{1},\omega_{1}),\dotsc,(\alpha_{d},\omega_{d})
. Then
I
is good if any injective map

f:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I,

with
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}\neq 0
,
admits an extension

g:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I

such that for each index
a
,
\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\subset\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
, and if

g_{a}(0)\leq\alpha_{a}\leq g_{a}(n_{a})
or
g_{a}(0)\leq\omega_{a}\leq g_{a}(n_{a})
, then, respectively,
\alpha_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
or
\omega_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
.

Let us first show how we can finish the proof of the pasting theorem using this result.

Proof of
4.3
.

Let
I
be a
d
-dimensional admittable pasting shape. If
I
is height
0
, then
I
is a cell and there is nothing to prove. Suppose that the statement holds
for any admittable pasting shape
I
of height
h
.

Let
I
now be an admittable pasting shape of height
h+1
with decomposition
(A,\{J_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m})
, where
A
is determined by a map

f:\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow A
. For
0\leq k\leq m
, consider the subshape

I_{k}:=A\cup\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}J_{i}\subset I.

Then it suffices to show that
I_{k}
is good for all
I
. We proceed by induction on
k
. Trivially, the pasting shape
I_{0}=A
is good since it is
(d-1)
-truncated. Suppose that
I_{k-1}
is good.
The subshape
J_{k}
has height
h
, hence is good by assumption. It follows that the pair

(I_{k-1},J_{k})
is a good division pair for
I_{k}
. Note that
I_{k}
has a decomposition given by
(A,\{J_{0},\dotsc,J_{k},\partial J_{k+1},\dotsc,\partial J_{m}\})
. Thus, in light of the factorization property of
3.2
,
any map
h:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I_{k}
, with
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}\neq 0
, admits an extension

g:\square[p_{1},\dotsc,p_{d}]\rightarrow I_{k}
such that

\mathrm{im}(g_{a})=\{t_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\mid h_{a}(0)\leq t_{a}\leq h_{a}(m_{a})\}\cup\mathrm{im}(h_{a}).

This is a desired extension
g
of
h
as in
4.2
, hence it follows from this result that
I_{k}
is good.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Suppose that
(K,J)
is a division pair for
I
. Then the spine inclusion for
I
factors
as the composite of inclusions

\mathrm{Sp}[I]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]\rightarrow[K]\cup[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]\rightarrow[I],

and the left map is a Segal equivalence if
(K,J)
is good.

Proof.

Suppose that all the distinct vertebrae of
K
are given by
V_{1},\dotsc,V_{k},\partial J
, and the vertebrae of
J
are given by
W_{1},\dotsc,W_{l}
.
Then the vertebrae of
I
are given by
V_{1},\dotsc,V_{k},W_{1},\dotsc,W_{l}
. Hence, the spine inclusion factors through the inclusion

[K]\cup[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]\subset[I]
. Note that we have pushout diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[K]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[K]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[I]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[K]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[I]\cup{[K]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I],}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[J]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[I]\cup[K]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[J]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[K]}\cup{[J]}\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I].}

If
(K,J)
is good, the left vertical maps in these diagrams are Segal equivalences, hence, the composite of the right vertical maps must then be a Segal equivalence as well.
∎

Example 4.16
.

The division pair
(\boxdot[i],A[i])
for
\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
associated to an injective map

i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}],

is good. Since

\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
is good,
4.2

implies that the inclusion

[\boxdot[i]]\cup[A[i]]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]]\rightarrow[\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]]

is a Segal equivalence.

Definition 4.17
.

Suppose that
(K,J)
and
(K^{\prime},J^{\prime})
are division pairs for, respectively,
d
-admittable pasting shapes
I
and
I^{\prime}
.
Then a
map of division pairs

f:(K,J)\rightarrow(K^{\prime},J^{\prime})

is a map of pasting shapes
f:I\rightarrow I^{\prime}
such that

f^{-1}(J^{\prime})=J,\quad\text{ and, }\quad f^{-1}(K^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}I=K\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}I.

In view of
4.2
, we have to show that the map appearing on the right is a Segal equivalence. This amounts to filling simplices (or rather: nerves of subshapes) that
do not appear in the domain of this map, using pushouts of Segal equivalences. We single out a class of pasting shapes that we will be able to fill:

Definition 4.18
.

Suppose that
(K,J)
is a division pair for
I
. We call a subshape
F\subset I

(K,J)
-fillable
if
F
is
(d-1)
-truncated or there exists a
d
-shaping map

f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow F

such that one of the two following conditions is met:

(1)

f
has image in
K
,

(2)

the composite

\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\xrightarrow{f}F\rightarrow I

underlies a map of division pairs

(\boxdot[i],A[i])\rightarrow(K,J)

for some injective map
i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
.

.

for
I
. The unique injective map
\square[1,2]\rightarrow F
underlies a map of division pairs
(\boxdot[i],A[i])\rightarrow(K,J)
, where
i:\square[1,1]\rightarrow\square[1,2]
selects
the top 2-box.

\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right).

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
(K,J)
be a division pair for
I
. Suppose that
F_{1},\dotsc,F_{n}
are
(K,J)
-fillable subshapes of
I
,
then the inclusion

\textstyle[K]\cup[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}[F_{i}]\cup[K]\cup[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]

is a Segal equivalence.

Its demonstration requires some preparation. The following is one of the crucial observations:

Proposition 4.21
.

Suppose that
(K,J)
is a division pair. Then for an
(K,J)
-fillable subshape
F
, the inclusion

[F\cap K]\cup[F\cap J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[F]\rightarrow[F]

is a Segal equivalence.

Proof.

If
F
is
(d-1)
-truncated or it satisfies condition (1) of

4.2
, this is clear, since then the left-hand side is the whole
[F]
and the inclusion in question is the identity. Thus we may focus on the case that there exists a
d
-shaping map
f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow F
(in particular,
f
is injective) that underlies
a map of division pairs

(\boxdot[i],A[i])\rightarrow(K,J),

for a suitable injective map
i
.
It then directly follows from the definitions that we obtain a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\boxdot]}\cup[A]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[\square]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F\cap K]}\cup[F\cap J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[F]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\square]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F]}.}

Since the map on the left is a Segal equivalence on account of
4.2
, the map on the right must also be a Segal equivalence.
∎

The final ingredient for the proof of
4.20
, is the following:

Proposition 4.22
.

The
(K,J)
-fillables for a division pair
(K,J)
are closed under taking intersections.

Lemma 4.23
.

Suppose that
I
is a pasting shape and that we have injective maps
f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I
and
g:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I
in
I
with intersecting images,
then one can construct a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[l_{1},\dotsc,l_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where all arrows appearing in the diagram are inclusions.

Proof.

Since the images of
f
and
g
intersect, we get that
\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\cap\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
is non-empty for each index
a
.
Let
h_{a}:[l_{a}]\rightarrow\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\cap\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
be the unique isomorphism for each
a
. These give
rise to maps
[l_{a}]\rightarrow\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\cap\mathrm{im}(g_{a})\rightarrow\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\cong[n_{a}]
, inducing a map

i:\square[l_{1},\dotsc,l_{d}]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
. Similarly, we obtain a map

j:\square[l_{1},\dotsc,l_{d}]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
. It can be readily checked that this gives a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[l_{1},\dotsc,l_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

To check that this square is a pullback square, it suffices to check this on the level of nerves as the nerve functor is fully faithful.
Thus we must verify that for any
([k_{1}],\dotsc,[k_{d}])\in\Delta^{\times d}
, the following
square is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}\Delta([k_{a}],[l_{a}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}\Delta([k_{a}],[n_{a}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}\Delta([k_{a}],[m_{a}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{I}(\coprodop\displaylimits_{1\leq a\leq d}[k_{a}],\mathbb{N}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits i_{a}\circ-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits j_{a}\circ-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits f_{a}\circ-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits g_{a}\circ-}

and this readily follows from the construction of the maps
i
and
j
.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
(K,J)
be a division pair for
I
. Suppose that the corners of
J
are given by
(\alpha_{1},\omega_{1}),\dotsc,(\alpha_{d},\omega_{d})
.
Then for an injective map
f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

f
underlies a map of division pairs

(\boxdot[i],A[i])\rightarrow(K,J),

(2)

for all
1\leq a\leq d
, we have
\max(\min(f_{a}),\alpha_{a})<\min(\max(f_{a}),\omega_{a})\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})
.

Proof.

First, suppose that (1) is satisfied so that we have an injective map

i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]

with the property that
fi
has image in
J
and
f
carries every non-degenerate
d
-box of
\boxdot[i]
to a non-degenerate

d
-box of
K
.
Define
\alpha_{a}^{\prime}:=f_{a}(i_{a}(0))
and
\omega_{a}^{\prime}:=f_{a}(i_{a}(1))
. From
3.2
,
it follows that

\alpha_{a}\leq\alpha_{a}^{\prime}<\omega_{a}^{\prime}\leq\omega_{a}
.
If
f_{a}(0)<\alpha_{a}
, then the box
(x,y)
given by
x_{b}=0
for all
b
and
y_{b}=n_{b}
if
a\neq b
and
y_{a}=i_{a}(0)
,
is a non-degenerate
d
-box in
M^{-}_{a}\subset\boxdot
.
Hence
(f(x),f(y))
is contained in
K
. Again by
3.2
,
we necessarily must have that
f(y)_{a}=\alpha^{\prime}_{a}\leq\alpha_{a}
.
Hence
\alpha_{a}=\alpha_{a}^{\prime}
. Thus we deduce that
\max(f_{a}(0),\alpha_{a})\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})
.
Similarly,
one deduces that
\min(f_{a}(n_{a}),\omega_{a})\in\mathrm{im}(f_{a})
.

Conversely, suppose that (2) is satisfied.
Let

i:\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]

be the injective inclusion that satisfies

f_{a}(i_{a}(0))=\max(f_{a}(0),\alpha_{a})<\min(f_{a}(n_{a}),\omega_{a})=f_{a}(i_{a}(1))

for all
a
.
If
(x,y)
is a box in
\square
, then
(f(x),f(y))
is contained in
J
if and only if
i_{a}(0)\leq x_{a},y_{a}\leq i_{a}(1)
for all
a
on account of
3.2
.
Hence,
f^{-1}(J)=A[i]
. Let
(x,y)
be a non-degenerate
d
-box of
\square
. Then we have to show
that
(f(x),f(y))
is in
K
if and only if
(x,y)
is in
\boxdot
. If
(x,y)\in\boxdot
, then there
exists some index
a
so that
x_{a}<y_{a}\leq i_{a}(0)
(or
i_{a}(1)\leq x_{a}<y_{a}
, but this case is handled similarly). Thus
f_{a}(x_{a})<f_{a}(y_{a})\leq f_{a}(i_{a}(0))
. Hence,
f_{a}(i_{a}(0))\neq f_{a}(0)
, so that we must have
f_{a}(i_{a}(0))=\alpha_{a}
. Thus
(f(x),f(y))\in K
. The converse implication is shown similarly, using
3.2
and the assumption that the
f_{a}
’s are injective.
∎

Corollary 4.25
.

Let
(K,J)
be a division pair for
I
. Suppose that the corners of
J
are given by
(\alpha_{1},\omega_{1}),\dotsc,(\alpha_{d},\omega_{d})
. Let
F\subset I
be a subshape that comes with a
d
-shaping map
f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow F
.
Then
F
is
(K,J)
-fillable if for each index
1\leq a\leq d
, we have that
\max(\min(f_{a}),\alpha_{a})
and
\min(\max(f_{a}),\omega_{a})
are in the image of
f_{a}
.

Proof.

Let us write
\alpha^{\prime}_{a}:=\max(\min(f_{a}),\alpha_{a})
and
\omega_{a}^{\prime}:=\min(\max(f_{a}),\omega_{a})
.
If
\alpha^{\prime}_{a}\geq\omega^{\prime}_{a}
for some
a
, then we must have that the image of
f
is either in
K
, or
f_{a}(0)=f_{a}(n_{a})
, in which case
F
is
(d-1)
-truncated. Otherwise,
we deduce that
f
meets condition (2) of
4.2
.
∎

Proof of
4.2
.

If
F
and
F^{\prime}
have empty intersection, or one of these subshapes is
(d-1)
-truncated, then we are done. Consequently,
we may assume there exist
d
-shaping maps
f:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I
and
g:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I

that witness, respectively,
F
and
F^{\prime}
to be
(K,J)
-fillable, and which have intersecting images.
On account of
4.2
, there is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[l_{1},\dotsc,l_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I,}

where all maps are injective.
Note that this implies that the map

h:\square[l_{1},\dotsc,l_{d}]\rightarrow F\cap F^{\prime}

is
d
-shaping. Indeed, suppose that
\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow F\cap F^{\prime}
is an injective map. Then
the composite
\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow F\cap F^{\prime}\rightarrow I
factors through
f
and
g
by assumption,
yielding a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[1,\dotsc,1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I,}

thus the desired factorization
\square[1,\dotsc,1]\rightarrow\square[l_{1},\dotsc,l_{d}]
exists in view of the universal property of the pullback.

If
f
or
g
has image in
K
, then
h
has so as well and we are done. Otherwise, condition (2) of
4.2
is met
for both
f
and
g
. Recall that by construction,

\mathrm{im}(h_{a})=\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\cap\mathrm{im}(g_{a}).

One now readily checks that
\max(\min(h_{a}),\alpha_{a})
and
\min(\max(h_{a}),\omega_{a})
are in the image of
h_{a}
again, where the
(\alpha_{a},\omega_{a})
’s are as in the statement of the cited lemma.
Thus
4.2
now assures that
h
again witnesses
F\cap F^{\prime}
to be
(K,J)
-fillable.
∎

We may now assemble our results to prove
4.20
:

Proof of
4.20
.

The inclusion

\textstyle[K]\cup[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}[F_{i}]\cup[K]\cup[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I]

is a pushout of the map

\textstyle\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}[F_{i}\cap K]\cup[F_{i}\cap J]\cup{\mathrm{tr}}_{\leq d-1}[F_{i}]\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}[F_{i}].

Thus it suffices to show that the latter inclusion is a Segal equivalence.
This follows if we can show that the set
S
of pairs of subsets of
[I]
consisting of

([F\cap K]\cup[F\cap J]\cup{\mathrm{tr}}_{\leq d-1}[F],[F]),

where
F\subset I
is
(K,J)
-fillable,
satisfies the conditions (1) and (2) of
2.2
. But condition (2) is precisely
4.2
and
condition (1) follows from
4.2
.
∎

We conclude by demonstrating how
4.2
follows from
4.20
, which then completes the proof
of all ingredients that went into the proof of pasting theorem
3.49
.

Proof of
4.2
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be the set of all subshapes of
I
which are
(K,J)
-fillable. In light of
4.2
and
4.20
, it is enough to show
that

\textstyle[I]=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{F\in\mathscr{F}}[F]\cup[K]\cup[J]\cup\mathrm{tr}_{\leq d-1}[I].

To this end, we have to show any injective map
f:\square[m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}]\rightarrow I
with
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}\neq 0
has image
in either
K
,
J
, or a fillable
F\in\mathscr{F}
.
By assumption, it suffices to check this for an extension
g:\square[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]\rightarrow I
of
f

such that for each index
a
,
\mathrm{im}(f_{a})\subset\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
, and if

g_{a}(0)\leq\alpha_{a}\leq g_{a}(n_{a})
or
g_{a}(0)\leq\omega_{a}\leq g_{a}(n_{a})
then, respectively,
\alpha_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
or
\omega_{a}\in\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
.
If
\max(g_{a}(0),\alpha_{a})\geq\min(g_{a}(n_{a}),\omega_{a})
for some index
a
, then
g
must have image in
K
.
Hence, we may assume that
\max(g_{a}(0),\alpha_{a})<\min(g_{a}(n_{a}),\omega_{a})
for all
a
. In this case, it follows from
the properties of the extension
g
that
\max(g_{a}(0),\alpha_{a}),\min(g_{a}(n_{a}),\omega_{a})\in\mathrm{im}(g_{a})
.
Thus the image of
g
is
(K,J)
-fillable in light of
4.2
.
∎

5.
Outlook: an
(\infty,d)
-categorical pasting theorem

Recall that
d
-uple Segal spaces may be used to model
(\infty,d)
-categories. For
d=1
, this model is due to Rezk
[
Rez01
]
, and the generalization
to higher dimensions
d>1
is due to Barwick
[
Bar05
]
. The pasting theorem that we have proven in this article, may thus
be specialized to a pasting theorem for
(\infty,d)
-categories. In this final section, we sketch an idea of how this can be achieved.

Following
[
Hau18
]
, let us first
recall Barwick’s model of
(\infty,d)
-categories via
d
-uple Segal spaces. A feature of
d
-uple Segal spaces is its many different directions of cells: there
are
d\choose k
directions for
k
-cells. However, a
d
-uple Segal space that models an
(\infty,d)
-category should only have non-trivial

k
-cells in one preferred direction. This is captured in the following definition:

Definition 5.1
.

Every (1-uple) Segal space is by definition a
1-fold Segal space
. Inductively, a
d
-uple Segal space
X

is called a
d
-fold Segal space
if
X_{0,\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}
is essentially constant and additionally,
X_{1,\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}
is a
(d-1)
-fold Segal space. We denote
the full subcategory of
\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
spanned by the
d
-fold Segal space by
\mathrm{Seg}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
.

It follows that the non-trivial
k
-cells of a
d
-fold Segal space
X
are concentrated in
X_{1,\dotsc,1,0,\dotsc,0}
. By definition, equivalences
in
\mathrm{Seg}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
are level-wise equivalences, and one can show that there are

d
-categorical equivalences (see
[
Hau18
, Remark 7.19]
) between
d
-uple Segal spaces that are not level-wise equivalences.
To remedy this, one needs to impose a further
completeness

condition:

Definition 5.2
.

A Segal space
X
is said to be
complete
if the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}\times X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}\times X_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\{0\leq 2\}^{*},\{1\leq 3\}^{*})}

of spaces is a pullback square. Here the bottom and top arrows are degeneracy maps, and the left arrow is the diagonal. Inductively, a
d
-fold Segal space

X
is called
complete
if
X_{\bullet,0,\dotsc,0}
and
X_{1,\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}
are complete
1
- and
(d-1)
-fold Segal spaces, respectively.

Remark 5.3
.

In the case that
d=1
, the definition of complete Segal spaces is due to Rezk
[
Rez01
]
.
For
d>1
, the definition of completeness coincides with Barwick’s original criterion
[
Bar05
]
(see also
[
BSP21
, Definition 14.1]
) on account of
[
Hau18
, Lemma 7.22]
.

It is a celebrated result of Joyal and Tierney that complete Segal spaces model
\infty
-categories:

Theorem 5.4

(
[
JT07
]
)
.

The restriction of the Yoneda embedding

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S}):\mathscr{C}\mapsto([n]\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n],\mathscr{C}))

is an equivalence onto the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
spanned by the complete Segal spaces.

In
[
BSP21
]
, Barwick and Schommer-Pries present an axiomatization of the
\infty
-category of
(\infty,d)
-categories and show
that the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Seg}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
spanned by the complete
d
-fold Segal spaces, satisfies these axioms. Henceforth, we will
write
\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,d)}
for this subcategory. Direct comparisons with a selection of other models for
(\infty,d)
-categories can be found in
[
BR13
]
,
[
BR20
]
, and
[
OR22
]
accompanied by
[
Lou22
]
.

Proposition 5.5
.

The inclusion of the full subcategory

\mathrm{Seg}^{d}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})

admits a left adjoint. In other words,
\mathrm{Seg}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
is a reflective subcategory of
\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
.

Proof.

This follows from the same arguments as in the proof of
[
BSP21
, Lemma 14.2]
.
∎

We may now consider the composite functor

\{-\}:\textup{Shape}^{d}\xrightarrow{[-]}\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}^{d}(\mathscr{S}),

where the latter functor is the left adjoint to the inclusion
\mathrm{Seg}^{d}(\mathscr{S})\subset\textup{Cat}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
. In order to get a meaningful pasting theorem for
(\infty,d)
-categories,
one should compute this functor. If
I
is a
d
-dimensional pasting shape, we expect that the coherence data of a map of
d
-uple Segal spaces from the nerve
[I]
to a
d
-fold Segal space,
is encoded by a complete and discrete
d
-fold Segal space associated to
I
. The simplices of this
d
-fold Segal space
should admit an explicit description that is reminiscent of
3
. This would then imply the following:

Conjecture 5.6
.

Let
I
be a
d
-dimensional pasting shape. Then the
d
-categorical nerve
\{I\}
of
I
is a complete and discrete
d
-fold Segal space.

Remark 5.7
.

Complete and discrete
d
-fold Segal spaces correspond to
gaunt
d
-categories
on account of
[
BSP21
, Corollary 12.3]
. Gaunt
d
-categories are
d
-categories in which every invertible cell is an identity, see also
[
BSP21
, Section 2]
.

Remark 5.8
.

As a stepping stone towards proving
5
, one could first consider the case that
d=2
. Then
a
2
-fold Segal space is precisely a
2
-uple Segal space where all vertical arrows are degenerate. Pictorially, if one starts with a 2-dimensional pasting shape
I

that is represented by a graph as in
3
, then the 2-categorical nerve of
I
would be the 2-category that is represented by the graph (with colorings)
that is obtained by contracting the vertical edges. For instance, one can compute that

\left\{\hbox to168.84pt{\vbox to87.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 84.41663pt\lower-43.97923pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{

Combining the conjecture with the pasting theorem we have proven in this article, we would gain a meaningful pasting theorem for
(\infty,d)
-categories:

Theorem 5.9
.

Suppose that
5
holds.
Let
I
be a
d
-dimensional locally composable pasting shape covered by subshapes
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}
. Then
I
can be written as

I=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}I_{i},

and this union is preserved by the
d
-categorical nerve functor so that the canonical map

\textstyle\colim_{J\in\mathcal{P}(\{I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}\})}\{J\}\rightarrow\{I\}

is an equivalence of
d
-fold Segal spaces between complete and discrete
d
-fold Segal spaces. In particular, it is an equivalence of

(\infty,d)
-categories.

Proof.

The main part follows from
3.49
and the fact that
left adjoints preserve colimits. The final assertion follows from the fact that the inclusion
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,d)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}^{d}(\mathscr{S})
is fully faithful, and hence
reflects colimits.
∎

*
